1981-separately from the rest of the network and use them to estimate

45-Avi Ostfeld, F.ASCE5; Shuming Liu, Aff.M.ASCE6; Marios Kyriakou7; Pavlos Pavlou8;
46-Mengning Qiu9; and Marios M. Polycarpou10
47-Abstract: A key challenge in designing algorithms for leakage detection and isolation in drinking water distribution systems is the
48-performance evaluation and comparison between methodologies using benchmarks. For this purpose, the Battle of the Leakage Detection
49-and Isolation Methods (BattLeDIM) competition was organized in 2020 with the aim to objectively compare the performance of methods for
50:the detection and localization of leakage events, relying on supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) measurements of flow and
51:pressure sensors installed within a virtual water distribution system. Several teams from academia and the industry submitted their solutions
52-using various techniques including time series analysis, statistical methods, machine learning, mathematical programming, met-heuristics,
53-and engineering judgment, and were evaluated using realistic economic criteria. This paper summarizes the results of the competition and
54-conducts an analysis of the different leakage detection and isolation methods used by the teams. The competition results highlight the need for
55-further development of methods for leakage detection and isolation, and also the need to develop additional open benchmark problems for this
56-purpose. DOI: 10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001601. © 2022 American Society of Civil Engineers.
57-Introduction
58-Drinking water distribution networks (DWDNs) are susceptible to
59-infrastructure failures, which may lead to water losses. The global
60:average nonrevenue water (NRW) is 30%, with an estimated annual
61:cost of USD 39 billion (Liemberger and Wyatt 2019). A significant
62-part of NRW is due to background leakages and pipe bursts, which
63-may occur anywhere within the distribution network. Background
64-leakages are typically difficult to detect because of their small
65-size, whereas pipe bursts are easier to locate because they are
66-of larger size and may appear on the surface. The early detection
--
68-duces the time required for addressing the event and therefore
69-reducing the risk of further infrastructure degradation, contamina-
70-tion events, and consumer complaints. Leakage diagnosis in water
71-distribution systems has attracted a great deal of attention from
72-both practitioners and researchers over the past years (Chan et al.
73:2018). The process of leakage diagnosis can be separated into
74-leakage detection, which focuses on identifying the existence of
75-a leak in the network; and leakage localization, which aims to
76-provide an approximate location of leakages given the available
77:measurements. A recent review paper (Chan et al. 2018) classifies
78-leakage detection methodologies into passive and active methods.
79-Passive methods (also referred to as equipment-based, hardware,
80-or external methods) require the deployment of specialized equip-
81-ment, such as acoustic sensors or ground-penetrating radars, at
82-areas that are suspected of leakage. Active methods (also referred
83-to as internal or software methods) are methods that are based on
84-the presence of permanently installed sensors that continuously
85-monitor the system for leakages. The latest developments in hy-
86-draulic sensor technology and online data acquisition systems
87-have enabled water companies to deploy a larger number of more
88:accurate pressure and flow devices with less cost. These data can
89-be used to monitor the system in real time and develop method-
90-ologies that use the data to detect and prelocalize leaks using ac-
91-tive methods. Prelocalization is the process of defining an area in
92-which the leak exists instead of pinpointing exactly its location.
93-This research area has witnessed a significant interest, as indicated
94:in recent review papers (Li et al. 2015; Chan et al. 2018; Zaman
95:et al. 2019).
96-The term model-based leakage diagnosis is used to describe
97-methodologies that utilize a model of the DWDN (also referred
98-to as a numerical model) and sensor measurements to estimate
99-the steady-state hydraulic conditions in the network (Vrachimis
100:et al. 2018b). The operating principle behind model-based leakage
101-detection, as suggested by Pudar and Liggett (1992), is to find

151-leakage diagnosis, methodologies for optimal placement of pres-
152-sure sensors are used (Farley et al. 2010; Casillas et al. 2013;
153-Cuguero-Escofet et al. 2017). Finally, the consideration of meas-
154-urement and model uncertainties is important when using these
155-methods to determine if the network is operating in a normal state
156:(Vrachimis et al. 2019) and should be taken into account before
157-making a decision about the occurrence of a leakage in the net-
158:work (Vrachimis et al. 2018a).
159-Leakage localization methods are typically model based be-
160-cause of the limited information provided by the small number of
161-sensors; one of the first representative examples is the work in Wu
162-et al. (2009), where the authors developed a model-based ap-
163-proach for leak localization that was applied to a large real system.
164-Another interesting model-based approach applied on real systems
165:is found in Sophocleous et al. (2019), where the authors formu-
166-lated an optimization problem to perform leakage diagnosis and
167-deal with the dimentionality of the problem using search space
168-reduction to reduce decision variables. Some approaches relate
169-the acquired measurements with the simulated output from many
170-simulated leakage scenarios on different locations of the network
171-(Farley et al. 2010; Goulet et al. 2013); the geographical mapping
172-of each model component can then be used to indicate the prob-
173-ability that a zone contains a leakage (Perez et al. 2014). Research-
174:ers have also used pressure residual analysis by creating a system
175:pressure sensitivity matrix to identify the location of leaks based
176-on the assumption of a single leakage occurring in the system
177-(Pérez et al. 2011; Cuguero-Escofet et al. 2017). A more recent
178-approach considers modeling uncertainties to create a set-bounded
179-model of the system and then incorporates sensor measurements
180-in an optimization-based framework to detect and prelocalize leak-
--
193-et al. (2002), where the authors introduced artificial neural net-
194-works (ANNs) for burst detection and continued to extend their
195-work in the following years (Mounce et al. 2010). Another ap-
196-proach is found in Eliades and Polycarpou (2012), where the au-
197-thors proposed an algorithm that analyzes the discrete inflow
198:signal of a district metered area (DMA) by using an adaptive
199-approximation methodology for updating the coefficients of a
200-Fourier series and detects leakages by utilizing the cumulative
201-sum (CUSUM) algorithm. Soldevila et al. (2016) used a mixed
202-model-based and data-driven approach to improve performance.
203-The study in Wu and Liu (2017) provides a review of data-driven
--
205-proaches are promising for use in real-life burst detection; how-
206-ever, reducing false alarms is still an important issue. Moreover, a
207-comprehensive performance evaluation procedure, especially
208-under different network configurations, might be necessary.
209-Leakage diagnosis methods are commonly evaluated on pri-
210:vate commercial data sets (Chan et al. 2018), and as a result it
211-is not possible to objectively compare different methods in their
212-ability to detect and isolate leaks. Moreover, data from real sys-
213-tems may not be readily available, while many aspects of the
214-system operation are unknown. For example, information about
215-the exact location, magnitude, and time profile of leakages is typ-
--
218-on real systems and having all the available information about the
219-system is the development of a realistic simulation benchmark
220-built on the expertise of practitioners, of which the operation re-
221-sembles that of a real system. Recently, a benchmark leakage de-
222-tection data set named LeakDB has been developed (Vrachimis
223:et al. 2018c); it was created using the Water Network Tool for
224-Resilience (WNTR) tool (Klise et al. 2017). The data set comprises
225:data generated from benchmark networks and uses pressure-
226-driven demands and realistic leakage modeling (van Zyl et al.
227-2017). In this work, a realistic open benchmark for leakage detec-
228-tion and localization is developed and used in a “battle” (Taormina
229:et al. 2018) to allow different teams to evaluate their methods in a
230-unified way.
231-The Battle of Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods
232-(BattLeDIM) was initially organized in 2020 as part of the Com-
233-puting and Control for the Water Industry and Water Distribution
234-Systems Analysis (CCWI/WDSA 2020) conference (which was
235-postponed due to the COVID-19 pandemic). The competition
236-aimed to objectively compare the performance of methods for the
237-detection and localization of leakage events, relying on supervisory
238:control and data acquisition (SCADA) measurements of flow and
239:pressure sensors generated using a realistic virtual city, which was
240:based on a real water distribution network in Cyprus. The overall
241-objective was to detect as many leakages as possible, as fast as pos-
242-sible, and as close to the source as possible, while avoiding false
243-alarms. Participants could use different types of tools and methods,
244-including (but not limited to) engineering judgment, machine learn-
245-ing, statistical methods, signal processing, and model-based fault
--
247-industry around the world submitted their solutions to the compe-
248-tition, and the results were presented on an online workshop organ-
249-ized on September 3, 2020.
250-The main contributions of this work are (1) to introduce a new
251-benchmark network named L-Town, developed for the purposes of
252:the competition, along with a benchmark SCADA data set; (2) to
253-provide an overview of the different leakage and isolation method-
254-ologies presented at the BattLeDIM competition; and (3) to analyze
255-their results with respect to different objectives by proposing a
256-comprehensive evaluation procedure.
257-L-Town Benchmark Network
258-In this section, we introduce a new benchmark water distribution
259-network, which we refer to as L-Town. This is a city-scale model
260:inspired by a coastal city in Cyprus that can be used for research
261-purposes. The network was suitably modified and redesigned for
262-security purposes. L-Town is part of the KIOS Virtual City Testbed,
263:an open software platform for simulating the SCADA operation of
264-different critical infrastructures, including water, power, telecom-
265-munications, and transportation systems.
266-© ASCE
267-04022068-2
268-J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.
--
272-
273---- PDF PAGE 5 ---
274-Topology and Structure
275-The L-Town model, depicted in Fig. 1, is represented using the
276-EPANET version 2.2 (Rossman et al. 2020) input file format. It
277:has 782 junctions and 905 pipe segments of approximately
278-50 m length each and delivers drinking water to around 10,000 con-
279-sumers and industries. It comprises a network of steel pipes with a
280-total length of 42.6 km and roughness coefficients (C values) be-
281-tween 120 and 140. The L-Town network has a loop ratio of 25%, a
282-measure of complexity when solving the hydraulics of the network;
283-it indicates that 25% of the pipes have to be removed to eliminate
284:all loops from the network (Vrachimis et al. 2019). The node el-
285-evations range between 1.5 and 75 m above the sea level.
286-The water distribution network of L-Town receives water from
287:two reservoirs, and it was designed to provide pressure head of at
288:least 20 m to all of its consumers. The normal operating pressure in
289:the network ranges between 20 and 30 m. A pressure reduction
290-valve (PRV) was installed at the lower part of the town (Area B)
291-to help reduce background leakages. The network has different
292:pressure areas, and therefore exhibits different sensitivity to leak-
293-ages. PRVs were also installed downstream of the two main reser-
294:voirs to help regulate the pressure. A pump and a water tank were
295-installed in the higher part of the town (Area C) to provide suffi-
296:cient pressure to the consumers of that area. The tank has a diameter
297-of 16 m with a cylindrical shape. The pump was programmed so the
298-tank refills during the night and empties to Area C during the day.
299-The design decision to include pipes of 50 m length was based
300-on the following considerations: First, it is common for a real net-
301-work to have consumer demand locations at a 50-m interval; thus,
--
338-where db
339-i = base demand of node i; n = number of consumer types;
340-αj
341-i = percentage of the jth consumer type at the ith node; βj
342-i =
343:average amount of water consumed in m3=h for each m2 of a
344-Fig. 1. L-Town benchmark network.
345-© ASCE
346-04022068-3
347-J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.
348- J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(12): 04022068 
--
352---- PDF PAGE 6 ---
353-building; and γi = total building area corresponding to node i. In
354-this benchmark, three types of consumers (n ¼ 3) were considered:
355-residential, commercial, and industrial.
356-Each node has a unique demand pattern for each consumer
357:type based on the statistical characteristics of real metered data
358-from the area. Specifically, a Fourier series model was used to
359:approximate the demands (Vrachimis et al. 2018c), capturing sea-
360-sonality (weekly, yearly) as well as the uncertainties on demand
361-patterns (Fig. 2). The overall water consumption is the linear com-
362-bination of the base demands with the corresponding patterns.
363-The demand peaking factor, which is the ratio of the maximum
364:daily demand (MDD) to the average daily demand (ADD) in a
365-water system, was also considered in the design of demand pat-
366-terns. The ratio, based on observations from real systems, typically
367-ranges from 1.2 for very large water systems to 3.0 or even higher
368-for specific small systems. The demand peaking factor in L-Town
369:ranges between 1.5 and 2.0, given it is an average size system.
370-BattLeDIM Challenge Scenario
371-As part of the Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation
372-Methods, all participating teams were given the following artificial
373-scenario to establish the challenge:
374-In previous years, the utility of L-Town was experiencing a
375-large number of pipe breaks and water losses, affecting its
376:service quality. During 2018, a number of leakage events oc-
377-curred, which were detected and fixed by the water utility.
378-However, it is believed that a number of smaller leakages oc-
379-curred but not revealed. It is also assumed that some leakages
380-occurred abruptly, whereas others developed gradually, as
381-incipient events, from background leaks into pipe bursts.
--
384-bution network, in which base demands were assigned to nodes,
385-following historical billing data of proximity consumers. Moreover,
386-two nominal demand patterns were identified for residential and
387-commercial consumer types (with some discrepancies). The utility
388-believes that there might be some inaccuracies in the model,
389:e.g., with respect to the pipe roughness and pipe diameters.
390-In addition, the utility was not able to confirm the status of all the
391-valves in the network (i.e., whether they are open or closed).
392-The L-Town water utility is searching for a solution to help
393:them analyze the SCADA dataset, and detect leakage events as
394-fast as possible. In addition, it is crucial for the utility to have
395-an indication where approximately the leakage occurs, so that
396-the field workers can inspect those potential leaks using their
397-equipment.
398-The L-Town utility has created an open call for teams to dem-
399-onstrate their ability in detecting and localizing leakage events.
400:The teams are given a historical SCADA dataset along with infor-
401-mation related with the leakages detected and fixed by the utility
402:throughout 2018, to use for training purposes and for calibrating
403-their models. It is possible that more leakage events occurred
404:during 2018, however the utility was not able to detect and local-
405-ize them.
406:Throughout 2019, the utility conducted periodic surveys using
407-additional sensing equipment, pipe inspections and other methods,
408-and was able to detect and isolate all the leakage events that oc-
409-curred within that period. The most critical of these events were
410-repaired, however it was not possible to repair some of these leak-
411-ages due to financial reasons.
412-The overall goal of this competition, is to identify methods
413-which are able to detect and localize the leakage events that oc-
414:curred in L-Town in 2019, as fast as possible (with respect to time)
415-and as accurately as possible (with respect to their location), in or-
416-der to minimize their overall financial costs, both in water losses, as
417-well as due to the hours spent in isolating the leakage by the utility
418-staff. The L-Town utility will compare the different solutions and
419-select the best one based on that objective.

1832-assumptions on cost may have on the ranking of solutions provided.
1833-The cost of water affects the economic score the most because this is
1834-proportional to the amount of water lost from leakages, while it does
1835-not affect the number of true positives or false positives achieved by
1836-each team. Five different water prices were used to reevaluate the

1969-approaches were used by the teams and the robustness of each
1970-approach to different evaluation functions may vary. Some of the
1971-observations are discussed in the following:
1972-•
1973-Most top-scoring teams made use of a nominal model, of which
1974:the parameters were calibrated in some form using sensor data,
1975-to construct a water distribution model that describes the normal
1976:operation of the system (such as Tsinghua, Under Pressure,
1977-IRI, and UNIFE) by incorporating existing leakages into the
1978-calibrated node demands. This allows the computation of the
1979:expected flows and pressures at different locations in the net-
1980:work. Moreover, they also consider the AMR measurements
1981-separately from the rest of the network and use them to estimate
1982-and calibrate demands.
1983-•
1984-For the detection of events, model-based residuals along with
1985-some form of change detection algorithm (e.g., Leakbusters,
1986:UNIFE, Under Pressure) or time series and signal processing
1987-(e.g., Tongji) analysis was preferred by most of the top-scoring
1988-teams. Some of these residuals were also utilized for localization
1989-purposes (e.g., IRI, Tsinghua).
1990-•
1991-For the leak isolation, top-scoring teams used some form of op-

2007-competition that aimed to objectively compare different methodol-
2008-ogies in their ability of detecting and isolating leakage events within
2009-a virtual water distribution system. For the purposes of this work,
2010-a new benchmark network was introduced, L-Town, based on a
2011-realistic water distribution system. Moreover, a synthetic 2-year
2012:SCADA benchmark data set was generated with leakages of various
2013-types and magnitudes, which can be used by the research commu-
2014-nity to develop leakage diagnosis methodologies, keeping in mind
2015-the limitations of this benchmark mentioned in “Limitations” sec-
2016-tion. An economic objective metric was defined to evaluate the
2017-different solutions, considering realistic operational costs. In total,

11-Kelsey Haddad29; Cynthia S. Lo30; Pratim Biswas31; M. Fayzul K. Pasha32; Bijay Kc33;
12-Saravanakumar Lakshmanan Somasundaram34; Mashor Housh35; and Ziv Ohar36
13-Abstract: The BATtle of the Attack Detection ALgorithms (BATADAL) is the most recent competition on planning and management of
14-water networks undertaken within the Water Distribution Systems Analysis Symposium. The goal of the battle was to compare the per-
15-formance of algorithms for the detection of cyber-physical attacks, whose frequency has increased in the last few years along with the
16:adoption of smart water technologies. The design challenge was set for the C-Town network, a real-world, medium-sized water distribution
17:system operated through programmable logic controllers and a supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) system. Participants were
18:provided with data sets containing (simulated) SCADA observations, and challenged to design an attack detection algorithm. The effective-
19-ness of all submitted algorithms was evaluated in terms of time-to-detection and classification accuracy. Seven teams participated in the
20-battle and proposed a variety of successful approaches leveraging data analysis, model-based detection mechanisms, and rule checking.
21-Results were presented at the Water Distribution Systems Analysis Symposium (World Environmental and Water Resources Congress)
22:in Sacramento, California on May 21–25, 2017. This paper summarizes the BATADAL problem, proposed algorithms, results, and future
23:research directions. DOI: 10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0000969. © 2018 American Society of Civil Engineers.

96-systems that combine physical processes with computation and net-
97-working: physical assets—such as pipes, pumps, and valves—work
98-in unison with networked devices that monitor and coordinate the
99-operations of the entire system. These devices include program-
100-mable logic controllers (PLCs), supervisory control and data
101:acquisition (SCADA) systems, remote terminal units (RTUs), static
102:and mobile sensor networks, and smart meters (Hill et al. 2014;
103:Gong et al. 2016; Sønderlund et al. 2016). The adoption of such
104-smart water technologies plays a pivotal role in enhancing the a
105-utomation and reliability of water distribution systems, but simul-
106-taneously exposes them to cyber-physical attacks (Rasekh et al.
107:2016)—namely the deliberate exploitation of computer systems
108-aimed at accessing sensitive information or compromising the op-
109-erations of the underlying physical system. Water (and wastewater)
110-systems represent one of the 16 critical infrastructure sectors iden-
111:tified by the US Department of Homeland Security (2017), accord-
112-ing to which the number of reported attacks on water infrastructures
113:has been growing steadily (ICS-CERT 2014, 2015, 2016), making
114-them the third most targeted sector after critical manufacturing and
115:energy (ICS-CERT 2016). To take remedial actions, several coun-
116-tries are establishing research centers and international collabora-
117-tions, such as the Israel–New York collaboration to defend water
118-systems from infrastructure terrorists (The Times of Israel 2018).
119-Protecting water distribution systems from cyber attacks re-
120-quires (as with other cyber-physical systems) a combination of pro-
121-active and reactive mechanisms (Cardenas et al. 2008). Proactive
122-mechanisms comprise all tools that reduce the chances to penetrate
123-the system, such as appropriate measures for traffic authentication
124-and confidentiality protection, access control, and device hardening
125:(Graham et al. 2016; Adepu et al. 2017). Because it is not possible
126-to rule out all attacks, cyber-physical systems should also be
127-equipped with intrusion detection schemes that assist with the re-
128-covery phase (Anderson 2010). Disclosing cyber attacks—without
129-issuing false alarms—is thus crucial. Unfortunately, this does not
130-come without some system-specific challenges. First, the definition
131-of anomalous behaviors should not only be related to point, or
132-content, anomalies—i.e., data points lying beyond some specific
133-thresholds—because cyber-physical attacks can tamper with one
134-or multiple network components while keeping the performance
135:characteristics within historical bounds (Abokifa et al. 2017). This
136-implies that detection schemes should be capable of disclosing both
137-content and contextual anomalies, namely, data points that are
138-considered abnormal when viewed against meta-information asso-
139-ciated with the data points (Hayes and Capretz 2015). For exam-
140-ple, unaccounted high volumes of water leaving tanks during the
141-night, when demand is generally low, may be seen as a contextual
142-anomaly revealed by looking at the flow data in the context of time.
143-Second, the same hydraulic response of a water network (e.g., low
144:water levels in a tank) can be obtained through different attacks
145:(Taormina et al. 2017). Therefore, detection schemes should also
146-identify the cyber components that have been attacked; this is a
147-nonnegligible challenge in large water networks. Third, all net-
148:worked devices, including SCADA systems, represent potential tar-
149:gets. This means that the information provided by SCADA systems
150-may not be fully reliable.
151-As the field of intrusion detection continues to grow, so too does
152-the need for an objective comparison of attack detection algorithms
153-for water distribution systems. The BATtle of the Attack Detection
154-ALgorithms (BATADAL) was organized for this purpose. Partic-
155:ipants were provided with data sets containing (simulated) SCADA
156-data for a water distribution system that was the target of cyber
157-attacks, and were tasked with the design of an attack detection
158-mechanism. The design goals of a detection algorithm were to:
159-(1) disclose the presence of an ongoing attack in the minimum
160-amount of time possible, (2) avoid issuing false alarms, and
--
163-contributed novel solutions, which were evaluated using specific
164-evaluation criteria—i.e., time-to-detection and classification accu-
165-racy. The BATADAL results were presented at a special session of
166-the Water Distribution Systems Analysis Symposium (World
167-Environmental and Water resources Congress), in Sacramento,
168:California on May 21–25, 2017.
169-This paper summarizes the main solutions and outcomes of the
170-BATADAL and proposes future research directions for event detec-
171-tion in the realm of cyber-physical security. The remainder of the
172-paper (1) describes the BATADAL problem, data, and evaluation
173-criteria; (2) presents a synopsis of the proposed attack detection
174-algorithms; (3) analyzes the results; and (4) presents conclusions
175-and future research directions.
176-Problem Description
177:The operators of the C-Town water distribution system have
178-observed anomalous behaviors in some hydraulic components,
179-e.g., tank overflows, reduction in pump speed, and anomalous
180-activation/deactivation of pumps. They suspect that the anomalies
181-are attributable to cyber-physical attacks that interfered with the
182-system operations and tampered with the readings recorded by the
183:SCADA system. The aim of the participants was to develop an at-
184-tack detection mechanism that detects the presence of attacks—in
185:the shortest amount of time—from the available hourly SCADA
186-data. In particular, attack detection algorithms must classify the sys-
187-tem state as either safe or under attack. The rest of this section sum-
188:marizes C-Town and presents the development data and evaluation
189-criteria. BATADAL rules, problem details, and data are available in
190-the Supplemental Data.
191:C-Town Network
192:The C-Town water distribution system is based on a real-world,
193-medium-sized network first introduced for the Battle of the Water
194-27Assistant Professor, Dept. of Electrical and Computer Engineering,
195-Univ. of Texas at San Antonio, San Antonio, TX 78249.
196-28Ph.D. Student, Dept. of Energy, Environmental, and Chemical Engi-
197-neering, Washington Univ., St. Louis, MO 63130-4899.
--
225-Water consumption is fairly regular throughout the year. These
226-physical assets are augmented with a network of 9 PLCs, which
227-are located in proximity to pumps, storage tanks, and valves. Most
228-of the PLCs controlling the pumps receive the information needed
229-by the control logic from other PLCs—for instance, PLC1 controls
230:Pumps PU1 and PU2 on the basis of Tank T1 water level, which is
231-monitored by PLC2 (Table 1). PLCs controlling pumps and valves
232-record information on the device status (on/off or open/closed),
233:the flow passing through it, and the inlet and outlet pressure of
234:pumping stations. The cyber network includes a SCADA system,
235-which coordinates the operations and stores the readings provided
236-by the PLCs. All information regarding the distribution system
237-were incorporated into the EPANET2 (Rossman 2000) input file
238:C-Town.inp which was provided to the participants. Water demand
239:in all nodes of C-Town was not shared, meaning that participants
240-could not run the model for the same period and then compare the
241:results with the provided SCADA data.
242-Development Data
243:Participants were provided with three data sets containing SCADA
244:readings for 43 system variables, i.e., tank water levels (7 variables,
245:denoted L_<tank id>), inlet and outlet pressure for one actuated
246-valve and all pumping stations (12 variables, denoted P_<junction
247-id>) and their flow and status (24 variables, denoted F_<actuator
248-id> and S_<actuator id>, respectively). All variables were continu-
249-ous, with the exception of the status of valve and pumps, repre-
250:sented by binary variables. The data sets were generated via
251-simulation with epanetCPA, a MATLAB toolbox that allows the
252-design of a variety of cyber attacks and the simulation, with
253-EPANET2 (version 2.0.12), of the hydraulic response of a water
254:distribution network (Taormina et al. 2017). The toolbox is avail-
255-able on GitHub (Taormina 2018). The hydraulic time step was set

300:to 15 min, although the SCADA data reported to the participants
301:were sampled with fixed hourly intervals. The first two data sets,
302:hereafter named Training Data Set 1 and Training Data Set 2, were
303-provided at the beginning of the competition, whereas the third
304:(Test Data Set) was subsequently used to evaluate and rank the at-
305-tack detection algorithms.
306-•
307:Training Data Set 1 was generated with a simulation horizon of
308:365 days. A key aspect of the data set was the absence of cyber
309-attacks, which made it suitable for studying the operations of the
310-water distribution system under normal operating conditions.
311-•
312:Training Data Set 2 contained 7 attacks, spanning 492 hourly time
313-steps. One attack was entirely revealed to the participants (by
314:appropriately labelling the corresponding time steps), whereas
315-the remaining attacks were either partially revealed or hidden
316-(Table 2). This corresponds to a postattack scenario in which for-
317-ensics experts carry out an investigation to determine whether,
318-when, and where the water distribution system has been affected.
319-•
320:Test Data Set contained 7 additional attacks, spanning over
321:407 hourly time steps (Table 3). Naturally, no information
322-regarding the attacks was revealed. Participants were required
323:to run the detection algorithms on Test Data Set and submit
324-a detection report containing the following information:
325-number of attacks detected, start and end time of each attack
326:(in DD-MM-YYYY HH format), and the label of the attacked
327-device(s) (optional).
328-The operations of the water system were altered through mali-
329-cious activation of hydraulic actuators, change of actuator settings,
330-and deception attacks—among the most common attacks on cyber-
331-physical systems (Cardenas et al. 2009). The latter were aimed at
332:manipulating the information sent or received by sensors and PLCs,
333-with the ultimate goal of affecting the operations of an actuator
334:(Urbina et al. 2016). Deception attacks were also used to alter the
335:information received by the SCADA system, therefore concealing
336:the real, physical outcomes of the attacks. SCADA concealment
337:was performed by either adding an offset to the transmitted sensor
338-readings or by replacing actual traffic information between the
339:PLCs and the SCADA system with previously recorded data, a type
340:of manipulation known as a replay attack (Urbina et al. 2016). The
341-replay attacks featured in the BATADAL consisted of replacing
342:data for a given hour of the day with those recorded during the same
343:hour 1 or 2 days previous. Fig. 2 illustrates Attack #3 (Training
344:Data Set 2), in which both pump operations and SCADA data were
345-compromised. In this case, a deception attack manipulated Tank T1
346:water level readings sent by PLC2–PLC1. PLC1 received a reading
347:of 0.5 m, which was below the low-level thresholds that activate
348-Pumps PU1 and PU2 (4 and 1 m, respectively). This resulted in
349-both pumps working for the entire period of the attack, which lasted
350:60 h. Consequently, the water level in Tank T1 reached the full tank
351:level (6.5 m), with the excess water being spilled. The adversary
352:tried to conceal the surge in T1 water level with a second deception
353:attack that altered the signal sent by PLC2 to the SCADA system
354-with a time-varying offset.

357-detection report submitted by each team against the provided Test
358:Data Set. The assessment was based on two scores that accounted
359-for (1) the time taken to detect an attack and (2) the classification

507-tion. The value of STTD varies between 0 and 1, with STTD ¼ 1 being
508-the ideal case in which all attacks are immediately detected and
509-STTD ¼ 0 being the case in which none of the attacks are detected.
510-Classification Performance
511-The accuracy of an algorithm was determined as its ability to
512-disclose threats without raising false alarms. In the context of
513-binary classification problems such as the BATADAL, the ability
514-Attack window

--
650-and to 1 and 0 in the second case. This highlights the contrasting
651-nature of the two components of SCLF and suggests how increased
652-sensitivity may come at the cost of issuing more false alarms (and
653-vice versa). Similarly, a potential conflict seems to exist between
654-ensuring a timely detection of the attacks (high STTD) and issuing
655:few false alarms, as pointed out by Housh and Ohar (2017c).
656-Attack Detection Algorithms
657-Seven teams participated in the BATADAL. This section briefly
658-describes each team’s attack detection algorithm.
659-•
660:Aghashahi et al. (2017) adopted a two-stage method that first
661-extracts a four-dimensional feature vector from the observed
662-(multidimensional) time series data and then constructs a clas-
663-sifier to detect attacks. In the first stage, the periods of attack/no
664-attack are used to extract four features that capture information
665-about the covariance and mean structure. For every time in-
666-stance, a local neighborhood is used to construct estimates of
667-mean and covariance. In the second stage, a supervised classi-
668-fication technique, random forests (Breiman 2001) is used to
669-classify the system state as safe or under attack.
670-•
671:Brentan et al. (2017) reduced the dimensionality of the problem
672:by exploiting the division of the C-Town network in district me-
673-tered areas (DMAs). For each DMA, they used data on normal
674-operating conditions to create recurrent neural networks that
675:forecast tank water levels as a function of pump flow, upstream
676:pressure (of the corresponding pump station), and hour of the
677:day (Díaz et al. 2016). A statistical control process identifies
678-abrupt changes in the neural network error time series when the
679-latter are applied to data containing cyber attacks (Guralnik and
680-Srivastava 1999). The rationale behind this approach is that it is
681-plausible to expect an increase in the error time series when the
682-system is under attack, because all neural networks are trained
683-with data pertaining to normal operations.
684-•
685:Chandy et al. (2017) developed two detection models running
686:sequentially. The first uses features of the SCADA data
687-(e.g., combined flow of pump stations or volume pumped
688-and stored) to check whether physical and/or operating rules
689:have been violated (e.g., tank levels within the bounds or
690-hydraulic relationships between nodes hold). The outcome of
691-this model is a set of flagged events, which are confirmed by the
692-second model. The latter is a convolutional variational auto-
693-encoder—belonging to the family of deep learning methods
694:(Kingma and Welling 2013; Doersch 2016)—that calculates
695-the reconstruction probability of the data: the lower the prob-
696-ability, the higher the chance of the data being anomalous.
697-•
698:Giacomoni et al. (2017) proposed two detection methods. The
699:first verifies the integrity of the actuator rules and SCADA data
700:by (1) checking whether the SCADA readings are consistent
701-with the actuator rules defined for the water distribution system
702-and (2) comparing the data for all variables to identify values
703-falling below or above thresholds created by analyzing data cor-
704-responding to normal operating conditions. The second method
705-builds on unveiling low-dimensionality components in the avail-
--
707-facilitating the separation of anomalies from the overall data.
708-The separation of data into normal and anomalous components
709-can be performed using prinicipal component analysis (PCA)
710-(Lakhina et al. 2004) or a convex optimization routine (Mardani
711-et al. 2013). The results reported subsequently for Giacomoni
712:et al. (2017) correspond to the second detection method based
713-on PCA.
714-•
715:Abokifa et al. (2017) introduced a three-stage detection method,
716-with each stage targeting a specific class of anomalies. The first
717-step features outlier detection techniques to find statistical out-
718-liers in the data, thereby focusing on local anomalies that affect
719:each sensor individually. The second stage employs an artificial
720-neural network—in the form of a multilayer perceptron—to
721-detect contextual anomalies that do not conform to normal
722-operating conditions. The third stage targets global anomalies
723:that simultaneously affect multiple sensors. To disclose these
724-anomalies, the layer uses principal component analysis to de-
725:compose the high-dimensional data sets of sensor measurements
726-into two subspaces representing normal and anomalous condi-
727-tions (Lee et al. 2013).
728-•
729:Pasha et al. (2017) presented an algorithm consisting of three
730-main interconnected modules working on control rules and
731-© ASCE
732-04018048-6
733-J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.
734- J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2018, 144(8): 04018048 
--
738---- PDF PAGE 7 ---
739-consistency checks, pattern recognition, and hydraulic and sys-
740-tem relationships. The first module checks the consistency of the
741-data against the set of control rules characterizing the water sys-
742-tem, whereas the second uses statistical analysis to identify pat-
743:terns for single hydraulic parameters and combinations thereof.
744-The idea is that patterns under cyber attacks may not follow the
745-original patterns. The anomalous behaviors detected by the first
746-two modules are finally confirmed by the third module, which
747-develops relationships for some physical quantities (e.g., tank
748:levels or flows) and compares their estimates against those
749-reported by the first two modules.
750-•
751:Housh and Ohar (2017b) proposed a model-based approach that
752-uses EPANET to simulate the hydraulic processes of the water
753-distribution systems, and then uses the error between EPANET-
754:simulated values and the available SCADA readings to detect
755-anomalous behaviors. The approach consists of three main steps:
756:first, available SCADA readings are used in a mixed-integer
757-linear program to estimate the water demand in all nodes of
758:C-Town; second, EPANET is used to generate reference values
759:for the SCADA readings which are used to produce simulation
760-errors when compared with actual readings; and third, a multi-
761:level classification approach is implemented to classify the ob-
762-tained simulation errors into event and normal conditions. A
763-similar approach was successfully developed by Housh and
764:Ohar (2017a) to detect contamination events in water distribution
765-systems.
766-Results
767-Algorithm Performance
768-Table 4 reports the values of the ranking, time-to-detection, and
769-classification scores (S, STTD, and SCLF) obtained by the competing
770:algorithms on the test data set. The table also reports the number of
771-attacks detected, the values of TPR and TNR yielding the classi-
772-fication score, and the elements of the confusion matrix (i.e., TP,
773-FP, TN, and FN). The scatter plot of Fig. 3 visually compares S,
774-STTD, and SCLF.
775-Fig. 3 highlights a cluster of four high-performing algorithms, all
776-achieving a ranking score S greater than or close to 0.90. The group
777:is led by the algorithm proposed by Housh and Ohar (2017b), which
778-showed the best overall performance (S ¼ 0.970). This algorithm
779-was the top scorer in terms of both time-to-detection, STTD, and clas-
780-sification score, SCLF. Indeed, the detection trajectory in Fig. 4(a)
781-shows that all attacks were immediately detected, with the exception
782:of the last one, which was disclosed a few hours after its starting
783:time. The algorithm of Abokifa et al. (2017) was a close second,
784-with S ¼ 0.949. This method was almost as quick as that of Housh
785:and Ohar (2017b) in identifying the attacks, but it was more prone to
786:false alarms. Abokifa et al.’s (2017) algorithm disclosed Attacks #10
787-and #11 as a single continuous episode, erroneously flagging the
788-system as under attack for the period between them [Fig. 4(b)].
789:The algorithm proposed by Giacomoni et al. (2017) had the same
790:TNR as that of Housh and Ohar (2017b), meaning that both algo-
791-rithms were the most successful in avoiding false alarms. However,
792:Giacomoni et al.’s (2017) algorithm was less sensitive, resulting in
793-lower TPR and minor timing errors [Fig. 4(c)] that led to a score of
794-0.927. With S ¼ 0.896, the algorithm proposed by Brentan et al.
795:(2017) can also be regarded as a strong performer. This algorithm
796-consistently and accurately detected most of the attacks, but it failed
797-to identify the last one [Fig. 4(d)].
798-Although outdistanced by the leading group, the contributions
799:of Chandy et al. (2017) and Pasha et al. (2017) were still sensibly
800-better than the naïve detection mechanisms described in section
801-“Problem Description.” Their scores were 0.802 and 0.773,
802-respectively. Figs. 4(e and f) show that these two detection algo-
803-rithms appear to suffer from opposite problems. The algorithm of
804:Chandy et al. (2017) was oversensitive—meaning that it was able
805-to identify most of the attack instances, but at the cost of issuing
806-numerous false alarms. This is reflected in a relatively high value
807-of the TPR, which, however, coincided with the lowest overall
808-value of the TNR. On the other hand, the algorithm of Pasha et al.
809:(2017) issued just a few false alarms, but it lacked sensitivity, thus
810-failing to flag the system as under attack for the entire duration
811-of events. This resulted in a very high value of the TNR and the
812-overall lowest TPR. Finally, the contribution of Aghashahi et al.
813:(2017) detected only three attacks, leading to a score of 0.534.
814-General Observations
815-The main insights from the results are summarized as follows:
816-•
817-All algorithms but one achieved a ranking score S greater than
818-0.75, meaning that they performed better than naïve detection
--
825-two metrics for most of the proposed algorithms (Fig. 3).
826-•
827-Interestingly, the BATADAL was won by the only model-based
828-approach. The idea of estimating the water demands to simulate
829-system dynamics with EPANET and then measuring the errors
830:with respect to the SCADA readings proved successful. In this
831-regard, it is important to note that the BATADAL demand pat-
832:terns were fairly regular and consistent across the three data sets.
833-Similarly, the participants were given the same computational
834:model of the C-Town network that was used to generate the
835:SCADA data (i.e., the input file C-Town.inp). Therefore, suc-
836-cessful application of this approach in real-world settings might
837-Table 4. Attack detection algorithms ranked by overall ranking score (S), assessed in terms of number of attacks detected; time-to-detection (STTD);
838-accuracy (SCLF); true positive ratio (TPR); true negative ratio (TNR); and number of true positives (TP), false positives (FP), true negatives (TN), and
839-false negatives (FN)
840-Rank
--
954-attack detection problems, although their performance would
955-probably vary with the modeling context at hand.
956-•
957-Only a few algorithms provided information on the attacked de-
958-vices. Among these, the algorithms proposed by Brentan et al.
959:(2017) and Giacomoni et al. (2017) were the most accurate.
960-•
961-Most teams presented multistage detection methods. Comparing
962-and confirming the detection issued by different modules can
963-help decrease classification errors.
964-•
965-Detection algorithms adopting a multivariate approach may be
966-better suited than algorithms analyzing a single time series per
967-time. The inherent interdependence of the elements in the water
968-network should theoretically allow for the detection of anoma-
969-lies even when adversaries try to conceal their actions by alter-
970:ing the SCADA readings of one or a few deployed sensors. Such
971-interdependence generally presents a nonlinear nature, which
972-can be well described by nonlinear models such as those belong-
973-ing to the class of artificial neural networks.
974-•
975-The adoption of supervised classification algorithms that learn
--
979-should always be combined with cross-validation schemes.
980-•
981-It appears that consistency checks and the analysis of control
982-rules should lead to the identification of the simplest attacks.
983-The results described previously were obtained using three spe-
984:cific data sets, which represent only a small portion of the entire set
985-of cyber attacks that could threaten a water distribution system.
986-Hence, the generation of different attacks is likely to produce differ-
987-ent results—a limitation observed in other battles (e.g., Ostfeld
988-et al. 2008).
989-Another factor that influenced the BATADAL results relates to
990-the evaluation criteria. First, the time-to-detection score STTD was
991-based on the ratio between the time taken to detect an attack and the
992-attack duration; this implies that a 2-h attack detected within 1 h
993:would have the same score as a 10-h attack detected in Hour 5.
994-Some operators may prefer to define scores that account explicitly
995-for the absolute value of the attack duration or its corresponding
996-damage. Second, the classification performance score SCLF is based
997-on TPR and TNR, which are common metrics for classification
998-problems. However, other metrics may be adopted, such as the
--

1026-(d)

1034-04018048-9
1035-J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.
1036- J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2018, 144(8): 04018048 
1037-Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by University of Texas at San Antonio on 06/11/18. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.
1038-
1039-
1040---- PDF PAGE 10 ---
1041-scenarios comprising varying hydraulic conditions (i.e., water
1042:demand or initial tank levels) and multiple attack sequences.
1043-•
1044:Use of real SCADA data: A major limitation of the current re-
1045-search in cybersecurity is the absence of detailed information
1046-about cyber attacks on water utilities (e.g., timing, compromised
1047-devices, or hydraulic response of the system). Access to such
1048:information and to the corresponding SCADA data—perhaps
1049-in some anonymized forms—would drastically enhance the un-
1050-derstanding of the skills and limitations of detection algorithms.
1051:Another challenge with SCADA data is that they often contain
1052-noise and measurement errors, so attack detection algorithms
1053-should be coupled with data preprocessing techniques.
1054-•
1055:Pressure deficient conditions and water quality problems: A lim-
1056-itation of this battle is its reliance on data generated with a
1057:demand-driven engine (Taormina et al. 2017). The range of at-
1058:tacks should be thus extended to include pressure-deficient con-
1059-ditions, water quality problems, and adversial attempts aimed at
1060-threatening emergency responses, such as firefighting opera-
1061:tions. In the absence of real SCADA data, simulated data could
1062-be generated by combining epanetCPA with more-sophisticated
1063-hydraulic engines (e.g., Sayyed et al. 2015) or water quality
1064-models, e.g., EPANET-MSX (Shang et al. 2007).
1065-•
1066-Sensitivity analysis: The definition of the cutoff criteria defining
1067-outliers regulates the trade-off between TPR and TNR for most
1068-of the algorithms, so there is a need to adopt or develop sensi-
1069-tivity analysis tools that draw the appropriate line between nor-
1070:mal and anomalous data (Abokifa et al. 2017). This step should
1071-always precede the application of an algorithm to new data
1072:sets—or its deployment in a SCADA system.
1073-•
1074-Computational requirements and scalability to large networks:
1075-The algorithms presented in this paper were applied to a
1076-medium-sized water distribution system comprising one
1077:SCADA system and nine PLCs. Because attack detection algo-
1078-rithm are meant to run in real-time, it is necessary to evaluate
1079-their computational requirements as well as their scalability to
1080-larger networks.
1081-•
1082-Attack localization: To facilitate and hasten incident resolu-
1083-tion, an ideal detection mechanism should be able to identify
1084-which components of the network are being attacked. This is a
1085-rather challenging task due to the intrinsic correlation among
1086-the hydraulic variables. For data-driven detection mechanisms,
1087-the task may be solved with variable (or feature) selection al-
1088:gorithms (Galelli et al. 2014; Karakaya et al. 2016), which
1089-identify the variables that are strongly related to the detected
1090-anomalies.
1091-•
1092-Integration with other fault detection mechanisms: Because at-
1093-tack detection mechanisms aim to disclose outliers and contex-
1094-tual anomalies in the system behavior, they may accidentally
1095-disclose anomalous behaviors that are not necessarily caused
1096:by cyber attacks (e.g., a water level sensor reporting wrong read-
1097-ings or a malfunctioning pump). Hence, there is a need to dis-
1098-close the nature of each problem being identified—for example,
1099-by combining the attack detection algorithms with fault detec-
1100-tion mechanisms that monitor the operations of PLCs.
1101-•
--
1109-sponding cost savings guaranteed by a detection algorithm.
1110-Closure
1111-The BATADAL was the first battle competition dealing with the
1112-emerging topic of cyber-physical security of water distribution sys-
1113-tems. This battle provided an opportunity to develop, test, and com-
1114:pare attack detection algorithms for SCADA data. The solutions
1115-provided by seven teams suggest that timely and accurate detection
1116-can be obtained by both model-based and data-driven approaches,
1117-usually made of multiple sequential stages. Although the data and
1118-algorithms presented here provide a first step toward an objective
1119-comparison of attack detection algorithms for water distribution
1120-systems, they do not represent the entire spectrum of modeling con-
1121-texts that practitioners and researchers would encounter. Hence, the
1122-authors hope that the availability of a dedicated website (BATADAL
1123:2017) will help share more data sets and case studies.

13-Abstract: The BATtle of the Attack Detection ALgorithms (BATADAL) is the most recent competition on planning and management of
14-water networks undertaken within the Water Distribution Systems Analysis Symposium. The goal of the battle was to compare the per-
15-formance of algorithms for the detection of cyber-physical attacks, whose frequency has increased in the last few years along with the
16:adoption of smart water technologies. The design challenge was set for the C-Town network, a real-world, medium-sized water distribution
17:system operated through programmable logic controllers and a supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) system. Participants were
18:provided with data sets containing (simulated) SCADA observations, and challenged to design an attack detection algorithm. The effective-
19-ness of all submitted algorithms was evaluated in terms of time-to-detection and classification accuracy. Seven teams participated in the
20-battle and proposed a variety of successful approaches leveraging data analysis, model-based detection mechanisms, and rule checking.
21-Results were presented at the Water Distribution Systems Analysis Symposium (World Environmental and Water Resources Congress)
22:in Sacramento, California on May 21–25, 2017. This paper summarizes the BATADAL problem, proposed algorithms, results, and future
23:research directions. DOI: 10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0000969. © 2018 American Society of Civil Engineers.

96-systems that combine physical processes with computation and net-
97-working: physical assets—such as pipes, pumps, and valves—work
98-in unison with networked devices that monitor and coordinate the
99-operations of the entire system. These devices include program-
100-mable logic controllers (PLCs), supervisory control and data
101:acquisition (SCADA) systems, remote terminal units (RTUs), static
102:and mobile sensor networks, and smart meters (Hill et al. 2014;
103:Gong et al. 2016; Sønderlund et al. 2016). The adoption of such
104-smart water technologies plays a pivotal role in enhancing the a
105-utomation and reliability of water distribution systems, but simul-
106-taneously exposes them to cyber-physical attacks (Rasekh et al.
107:2016)—namely the deliberate exploitation of computer systems
108-aimed at accessing sensitive information or compromising the op-
109-erations of the underlying physical system. Water (and wastewater)
110-systems represent one of the 16 critical infrastructure sectors iden-
111:tified by the US Department of Homeland Security (2017), accord-
112-ing to which the number of reported attacks on water infrastructures
113:has been growing steadily (ICS-CERT 2014, 2015, 2016), making
114-them the third most targeted sector after critical manufacturing and
115:energy (ICS-CERT 2016). To take remedial actions, several coun-
116-tries are establishing research centers and international collabora-
117-tions, such as the Israel–New York collaboration to defend water
118-systems from infrastructure terrorists (The Times of Israel 2018).
119-Protecting water distribution systems from cyber attacks re-
120-quires (as with other cyber-physical systems) a combination of pro-
121-active and reactive mechanisms (Cardenas et al. 2008). Proactive
122-mechanisms comprise all tools that reduce the chances to penetrate
123-the system, such as appropriate measures for traffic authentication
124-and confidentiality protection, access control, and device hardening
125:(Graham et al. 2016; Adepu et al. 2017). Because it is not possible
126-to rule out all attacks, cyber-physical systems should also be
127-equipped with intrusion detection schemes that assist with the re-
128-covery phase (Anderson 2010). Disclosing cyber attacks—without
129-issuing false alarms—is thus crucial. Unfortunately, this does not
130-come without some system-specific challenges. First, the definition
131-of anomalous behaviors should not only be related to point, or
132-content, anomalies—i.e., data points lying beyond some specific
133-thresholds—because cyber-physical attacks can tamper with one
134-or multiple network components while keeping the performance
135:characteristics within historical bounds (Abokifa et al. 2017). This
136-implies that detection schemes should be capable of disclosing both
137-content and contextual anomalies, namely, data points that are
138-considered abnormal when viewed against meta-information asso-
139-ciated with the data points (Hayes and Capretz 2015). For exam-
140-ple, unaccounted high volumes of water leaving tanks during the
141-night, when demand is generally low, may be seen as a contextual
142-anomaly revealed by looking at the flow data in the context of time.
143-Second, the same hydraulic response of a water network (e.g., low
144:water levels in a tank) can be obtained through different attacks
145:(Taormina et al. 2017). Therefore, detection schemes should also
146-identify the cyber components that have been attacked; this is a
147-nonnegligible challenge in large water networks. Third, all net-
148:worked devices, including SCADA systems, represent potential tar-
149:gets. This means that the information provided by SCADA systems
150-may not be fully reliable.
151-As the field of intrusion detection continues to grow, so too does
152-the need for an objective comparison of attack detection algorithms
153-for water distribution systems. The BATtle of the Attack Detection
154-ALgorithms (BATADAL) was organized for this purpose. Partic-
155:ipants were provided with data sets containing (simulated) SCADA
156-data for a water distribution system that was the target of cyber
157-attacks, and were tasked with the design of an attack detection
158-mechanism. The design goals of a detection algorithm were to:
159-(1) disclose the presence of an ongoing attack in the minimum
160-amount of time possible, (2) avoid issuing false alarms, and
--
163-contributed novel solutions, which were evaluated using specific
164-evaluation criteria—i.e., time-to-detection and classification accu-
165-racy. The BATADAL results were presented at a special session of
166-the Water Distribution Systems Analysis Symposium (World
167-Environmental and Water resources Congress), in Sacramento,
168:California on May 21–25, 2017.
169-This paper summarizes the main solutions and outcomes of the
170-BATADAL and proposes future research directions for event detec-
171-tion in the realm of cyber-physical security. The remainder of the
172-paper (1) describes the BATADAL problem, data, and evaluation
173-criteria; (2) presents a synopsis of the proposed attack detection
174-algorithms; (3) analyzes the results; and (4) presents conclusions
175-and future research directions.
176-Problem Description
177:The operators of the C-Town water distribution system have
178-observed anomalous behaviors in some hydraulic components,
179-e.g., tank overflows, reduction in pump speed, and anomalous
180-activation/deactivation of pumps. They suspect that the anomalies
181-are attributable to cyber-physical attacks that interfered with the
182-system operations and tampered with the readings recorded by the
183:SCADA system. The aim of the participants was to develop an at-
184-tack detection mechanism that detects the presence of attacks—in
185:the shortest amount of time—from the available hourly SCADA
186-data. In particular, attack detection algorithms must classify the sys-
187-tem state as either safe or under attack. The rest of this section sum-
188:marizes C-Town and presents the development data and evaluation
189-criteria. BATADAL rules, problem details, and data are available in
190-the Supplemental Data.
191:C-Town Network
192:The C-Town water distribution system is based on a real-world,
193-medium-sized network first introduced for the Battle of the Water
194-27Assistant Professor, Dept. of Electrical and Computer Engineering,
195-Univ. of Texas at San Antonio, San Antonio, TX 78249.
196-28Ph.D. Student, Dept. of Energy, Environmental, and Chemical Engi-
197-neering, Washington Univ., St. Louis, MO 63130-4899.
--
225-Water consumption is fairly regular throughout the year. These
226-physical assets are augmented with a network of 9 PLCs, which
227-are located in proximity to pumps, storage tanks, and valves. Most
228-of the PLCs controlling the pumps receive the information needed
229-by the control logic from other PLCs—for instance, PLC1 controls
230:Pumps PU1 and PU2 on the basis of Tank T1 water level, which is
231-monitored by PLC2 (Table 1). PLCs controlling pumps and valves
232-record information on the device status (on/off or open/closed),
233:the flow passing through it, and the inlet and outlet pressure of
234:pumping stations. The cyber network includes a SCADA system,
235-which coordinates the operations and stores the readings provided
236-by the PLCs. All information regarding the distribution system
237-were incorporated into the EPANET2 (Rossman 2000) input file
238:C-Town.inp which was provided to the participants. Water demand
239:in all nodes of C-Town was not shared, meaning that participants
240-could not run the model for the same period and then compare the
241:results with the provided SCADA data.
242-Development Data
243:Participants were provided with three data sets containing SCADA
244:readings for 43 system variables, i.e., tank water levels (7 variables,
245:denoted L_<tank id>), inlet and outlet pressure for one actuated
246-valve and all pumping stations (12 variables, denoted P_<junction
247-id>) and their flow and status (24 variables, denoted F_<actuator
248-id> and S_<actuator id>, respectively). All variables were continu-
249-ous, with the exception of the status of valve and pumps, repre-
250:sented by binary variables. The data sets were generated via
251-simulation with epanetCPA, a MATLAB toolbox that allows the
252-design of a variety of cyber attacks and the simulation, with
253-EPANET2 (version 2.0.12), of the hydraulic response of a water
254:distribution network (Taormina et al. 2017). The toolbox is avail-
255-able on GitHub (Taormina 2018). The hydraulic time step was set

306-•
307:Training Data Set 1 was generated with a simulation horizon of
308:365 days. A key aspect of the data set was the absence of cyber
309-attacks, which made it suitable for studying the operations of the
310-water distribution system under normal operating conditions.
311-•
312:Training Data Set 2 contained 7 attacks, spanning 492 hourly time
313-steps. One attack was entirely revealed to the participants (by
314:appropriately labelling the corresponding time steps), whereas
315-the remaining attacks were either partially revealed or hidden
316-(Table 2). This corresponds to a postattack scenario in which for-
317-ensics experts carry out an investigation to determine whether,
318-when, and where the water distribution system has been affected.
319-•
320:Test Data Set contained 7 additional attacks, spanning over
321:407 hourly time steps (Table 3). Naturally, no information
322-regarding the attacks was revealed. Participants were required
323:to run the detection algorithms on Test Data Set and submit
324-a detection report containing the following information:
325-number of attacks detected, start and end time of each attack
326:(in DD-MM-YYYY HH format), and the label of the attacked
327-device(s) (optional).
328-The operations of the water system were altered through mali-
329-cious activation of hydraulic actuators, change of actuator settings,
330-and deception attacks—among the most common attacks on cyber-
331-physical systems (Cardenas et al. 2009). The latter were aimed at
332:manipulating the information sent or received by sensors and PLCs,
333-with the ultimate goal of affecting the operations of an actuator
334:(Urbina et al. 2016). Deception attacks were also used to alter the
335:information received by the SCADA system, therefore concealing
336:the real, physical outcomes of the attacks. SCADA concealment
337:was performed by either adding an offset to the transmitted sensor
338-readings or by replacing actual traffic information between the
339:PLCs and the SCADA system with previously recorded data, a type
340:of manipulation known as a replay attack (Urbina et al. 2016). The
341-replay attacks featured in the BATADAL consisted of replacing
342:data for a given hour of the day with those recorded during the same
343:hour 1 or 2 days previous. Fig. 2 illustrates Attack #3 (Training
344:Data Set 2), in which both pump operations and SCADA data were
345-compromised. In this case, a deception attack manipulated Tank T1
346:water level readings sent by PLC2–PLC1. PLC1 received a reading
347:of 0.5 m, which was below the low-level thresholds that activate
348-Pumps PU1 and PU2 (4 and 1 m, respectively). This resulted in
349-both pumps working for the entire period of the attack, which lasted
350:60 h. Consequently, the water level in Tank T1 reached the full tank
351:level (6.5 m), with the excess water being spilled. The adversary
352:tried to conceal the surge in T1 water level with a second deception
353:attack that altered the signal sent by PLC2 to the SCADA system
354-with a time-varying offset.

359-for (1) the time taken to detect an attack and (2) the classification

975-The adoption of supervised classification algorithms that learn
--
979-should always be combined with cross-validation schemes.
980-•
981-It appears that consistency checks and the analysis of control
982-rules should lead to the identification of the simplest attacks.
983-The results described previously were obtained using three spe-
984:cific data sets, which represent only a small portion of the entire set
985-of cyber attacks that could threaten a water distribution system.
986-Hence, the generation of different attacks is likely to produce differ-
987-ent results—a limitation observed in other battles (e.g., Ostfeld
988-et al. 2008).
989-Another factor that influenced the BATADAL results relates to
990-the evaluation criteria. First, the time-to-detection score STTD was
991-based on the ratio between the time taken to detect an attack and the
992-attack duration; this implies that a 2-h attack detected within 1 h
993:would have the same score as a 10-h attack detected in Hour 5.
994-Some operators may prefer to define scores that account explicitly
995-for the absolute value of the attack duration or its corresponding
996-damage. Second, the classification performance score SCLF is based
997-on TPR and TNR, which are common metrics for classification
998-problems. However, other metrics may be adopted, such as the
--

1026-(d)
1027-(e)
1028-(f)
1029-(g)

1031:attack detection algorithm: (a) Housh and Ohar (2017b); (b) Abokifa et al. (2017); (c) Giacomoni et al. (2017); (d) Brentan et al. (2017); (e) Chandy
1032:et al. (2017); (f) Pasha et al. (2017); and (g) Aghashahi et al. (2017).

1040---- PDF PAGE 10 ---
1041-scenarios comprising varying hydraulic conditions (i.e., water
1042:demand or initial tank levels) and multiple attack sequences.
1043-•
1044:Use of real SCADA data: A major limitation of the current re-
1045-search in cybersecurity is the absence of detailed information
1046-about cyber attacks on water utilities (e.g., timing, compromised
1047-devices, or hydraulic response of the system). Access to such
1048:information and to the corresponding SCADA data—perhaps
1049-in some anonymized forms—would drastically enhance the un-
1050-derstanding of the skills and limitations of detection algorithms.
1051:Another challenge with SCADA data is that they often contain
1052-noise and measurement errors, so attack detection algorithms
1053-should be coupled with data preprocessing techniques.
1054-•
1055:Pressure deficient conditions and water quality problems: A lim-
1056-itation of this battle is its reliance on data generated with a
1057:demand-driven engine (Taormina et al. 2017). The range of at-
1058:tacks should be thus extended to include pressure-deficient con-
1059-ditions, water quality problems, and adversial attempts aimed at
1060-threatening emergency responses, such as firefighting opera-
1061:tions. In the absence of real SCADA data, simulated data could
1062-be generated by combining epanetCPA with more-sophisticated
1063-hydraulic engines (e.g., Sayyed et al. 2015) or water quality
1064-models, e.g., EPANET-MSX (Shang et al. 2007).
1065-•
1066-Sensitivity analysis: The definition of the cutoff criteria defining
1067-outliers regulates the trade-off between TPR and TNR for most
1068-of the algorithms, so there is a need to adopt or develop sensi-
1069-tivity analysis tools that draw the appropriate line between nor-
1070:mal and anomalous data (Abokifa et al. 2017). This step should
1071-always precede the application of an algorithm to new data
1072:sets—or its deployment in a SCADA system.
1073-•
1074-Computational requirements and scalability to large networks:
1075-The algorithms presented in this paper were applied to a
1076-medium-sized water distribution system comprising one
1077:SCADA system and nine PLCs. Because attack detection algo-
1078-rithm are meant to run in real-time, it is necessary to evaluate
1079-their computational requirements as well as their scalability to
1080-larger networks.
1081-•
1082-Attack localization: To facilitate and hasten incident resolu-
1083-tion, an ideal detection mechanism should be able to identify
1084-which components of the network are being attacked. This is a
1085-rather challenging task due to the intrinsic correlation among
1086-the hydraulic variables. For data-driven detection mechanisms,
1087-the task may be solved with variable (or feature) selection al-
1088:gorithms (Galelli et al. 2014; Karakaya et al. 2016), which
1089-identify the variables that are strongly related to the detected
1090-anomalies.
1091-•
1092-Integration with other fault detection mechanisms: Because at-
1093-tack detection mechanisms aim to disclose outliers and contex-
1094-tual anomalies in the system behavior, they may accidentally
1095-disclose anomalous behaviors that are not necessarily caused
1096:by cyber attacks (e.g., a water level sensor reporting wrong read-
1097-ings or a malfunctioning pump). Hence, there is a need to dis-
1098-close the nature of each problem being identified—for example,
1099-by combining the attack detection algorithms with fault detec-
1100-tion mechanisms that monitor the operations of PLCs.
1101-•
--
1109-sponding cost savings guaranteed by a detection algorithm.
1110-Closure
1111-The BATADAL was the first battle competition dealing with the
1112-emerging topic of cyber-physical security of water distribution sys-
1113-tems. This battle provided an opportunity to develop, test, and com-
1114:pare attack detection algorithms for SCADA data. The solutions
1115-provided by seven teams suggest that timely and accurate detection
1116-can be obtained by both model-based and data-driven approaches,
1117-usually made of multiple sequential stages. Although the data and
1118-algorithms presented here provide a first step toward an objective
1119-comparison of attack detection algorithms for water distribution
1120-systems, they do not represent the entire spectrum of modeling con-
1121-texts that practitioners and researchers would encounter. Hence, the
1122-authors hope that the availability of a dedicated website (BATADAL
1123:2017) will help share more data sets and case studies.
