This paper is part of the Journal of Water Resources\\r\\n135-Planning and Management, © ASCE, ISSN 0733-9496.\\r\\n--\\n151-leakage diagnosis, methodologies for optimal placement of pres-\\r\\n152-sure sensors are used (Farley et al. 2010; Casillas et al. 2013;\\r\\n153-Cuguero-Escofet et al. 2017). Finally, the consideration of meas-\\r\\n154-urement and model uncertainties is important when using these\\r\\n155-methods to determine if the network is operating in a normal state\\r\\n156:(Vrachimis et al. 2019) and should be taken into account before\\r\\n157-making a decision about the occurrence of a leakage in the net-\\r\\n158:work (Vrachimis et al. 2018a).\\r\\n159-Leakage localization methods are typically model based be-\\r\\n160-cause of the limited information provided by the small number of\\r\\n161-sensors; one of the first representative examples is the work in Wu\\r\\n162-et al. (2009), where the authors developed a model-based ap-\\r\\n163-proach for leak localization that was applied to a large real system.\\r\\n164-Another interesting model-based approach applied on real systems\\r\\n165:is found in Sophocleous et al. (2019), where the authors formu-\\r\\n166-lated an optimization problem to perform leakage diagnosis and\\r\\n167-deal with the dimentionality of the problem using search space\\r\\n168-reduction to reduce decision variables. Some approaches relate\\r\\n169-the acquired measurements with the simulated output from many\\r\\n170-simulated leakage scenarios on different locations of the network\\r\\n171-(Farley et al. 2010; Goulet et al. 2013); the geographical mapping\\r\\n172-of each model component can then be used to indicate the prob-\\r\\n173-ability that a zone contains a leakage (Perez et al. 2014). Research-\\r\\n174:ers have also used pressure residual analysis by creating a system\\r\\n175:pressure sensitivity matrix to identify the location of leaks based\\r\\n176-on the assumption of a single leakage occurring in the system\\r\\n177-(Pérez et al. 2011; Cuguero-Escofet et al. 2017). A more recent\\r\\n178-approach considers modeling uncertainties to create a set-bounded\\r\\n179-model of the system and then incorporates sensor measurements\\r\\n180-in an optimization-based framework to detect and prelocalize leak-\\r\\n--\\n193-et al. (2002), where the authors introduced artificial neural net-\\r\\n194-works (ANNs) for burst detection and continued to extend their\\r\\n195-work in the following years (Mounce et al. 2010). Another ap-\\r\\n196-proach is found in Eliades and Polycarpou (2012), where the au-\\r\\n197-thors proposed an algorithm that analyzes the discrete inflow\\r\\n198:signal of a district metered area (DMA) by using an adaptive\\r\\n199-approximation methodology for updating the coefficients of a\\r\\n200-Fourier series and detects leakages by utilizing the cumulative\\r\\n201-sum (CUSUM) algorithm. Soldevila et al. (2016) used a mixed\\r\\n202-model-based and data-driven approach to improve performance.\\r\\n203-The study in Wu and Liu (2017) provides a review of data-driven\\r\\n--\\n205-proaches are promising for use in real-life burst detection; how-\\r\\n206-ever, reducing false alarms is still an important issue. Moreover, a\\r\\n207-comprehensive performance evaluation procedure, especially\\r\\n208-under different network configurations, might be necessary.\\r\\n209-Leakage diagnosis methods are commonly evaluated on pri-\\r\\n210:vate commercial data sets (Chan et al. 2018), and as a result it\\r\\n211-is not possible to objectively compare different methods in their\\r\\n212-ability to detect and isolate leaks. Moreover, data from real sys-\\r\\n213-tems may not be readily available, while many aspects of the\\r\\n214-system operation are unknown. For example, information about\\r\\n215-the exact location, magnitude, and time profile of leakages is typ-\\r\\n--\\n218-on real systems and having all the available information about the\\r\\n219-system is the development of a realistic simulation benchmark\\r\\n220-built on the expertise of practitioners, of which the operation re-\\r\\n221-sembles that of a real system. Recently, a benchmark leakage de-\\r\\n222-tection data set named LeakDB has been developed (Vrachimis\\r\\n223:et al. 2018c); it was created using the Water Network Tool for\\r\\n224-Resilience (WNTR) tool (Klise et al. 2017). The data set comprises\\r\\n225:data generated from benchmark networks and uses pressure-\\r\\n226-driven demands and realistic leakage modeling (van Zyl et al.\\r\\n227-2017). In this work, a realistic open benchmark for leakage detec-\\r\\n228-tion and localization is developed and used in a “battle” (Taormina\\r\\n229:et al. 2018) to allow different teams to evaluate their methods in a\\r\\n230-unified way.\\r\\n231-The Battle of Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods\\r\\n232-(BattLeDIM) was initially organized in 2020 as part of the Com-\\r\\n233-puting and Control for the Water Industry and Water Distribution\\r\\n234-Systems Analysis (CCWI/WDSA 2020) conference (which was\\r\\n235-postponed due to the COVID-19 pandemic). The competition\\r\\n236-aimed to objectively compare the performance of methods for the\\r\\n237-detection and localization of leakage events, relying on supervisory\\r\\n238:control and data acquisition (SCADA) measurements of flow and\\r\\n239:pressure sensors generated using a realistic virtual city, which was\\r\\n240:based on a real water distribution network in Cyprus. The overall\\r\\n241-objective was to detect as many leakages as possible, as fast as pos-\\r\\n242-sible, and as close to the source as possible, while avoiding false\\r\\n243-alarms. Participants could use different types of tools and methods,\\r\\n244-including (but not limited to) engineering judgment, machine learn-\\r\\n245-ing, statistical methods, signal processing, and model-based fault\\r\\n--\\n247-industry around the world submitted their solutions to the compe-\\r\\n248-tition, and the results were presented on an online workshop organ-\\r\\n249-ized on September 3, 2020.\\r\\n250-The main contributions of this work are (1) to introduce a new\\r\\n251-benchmark network named L-Town, developed for the purposes of\\r\\n252:the competition, along with a benchmark SCADA data set; (2) to\\r\\n253-provide an overview of the different leakage and isolation method-\\r\\n254-ologies presented at the BattLeDIM competition; and (3) to analyze\\r\\n255-their results with respect to different objectives by proposing a\\r\\n256-comprehensive evaluation procedure.\\r\\n257-L-Town Benchmark Network\\r\\n258-In this section, we introduce a new benchmark water distribution\\r\\n259-network, which we refer to as L-Town. This is a city-scale model\\r\\n260:inspired by a coastal city in Cyprus that can be used for research\\r\\n261-purposes. The network was suitably modified and redesigned for\\r\\n262-security purposes. L-Town is part of the KIOS Virtual City Testbed,\\r\\n263:an open software platform for simulating the SCADA operation of\\r\\n264-different critical infrastructures, including water, power, telecom-\\r\\n265-munications, and transportation systems.\\r\\n266-© ASCE\\r\\n267-04022068-2\\r\\n268-J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\\r\\n--\\n272-\\r\\n273---- PDF PAGE 5 ---\\r\\n274-Topology and Structure\\r\\n275-The L-Town model, depicted in Fig. 1, is represented using the\\r\\n276-EPANET version 2.2 (Rossman et al. 2020) input file format. It\\r\\n277:has 782 junctions and 905 pipe segments of approximately\\r\\n278-50 m length each and delivers drinking water to around 10,000 con-\\r\\n279-sumers and industries. It comprises a network of steel pipes with a\\r\\n280-total length of 42.6 km and roughness coefficients (C values) be-\\r\\n281-tween 120 and 140. The L-Town network has a loop ratio of 25%, a\\r\\n282-measure of complexity when solving the hydraulics of the network;\\r\\n283-it indicates that 25% of the pipes have to be removed to eliminate\\r\\n284:all loops from the network (Vrachimis et al. 2019). The node el-\\r\\n285-evations range between 1.5 and 75 m above the sea level.\\r\\n286-The water distribution network of L-Town receives water from\\r\\n287:two reservoirs, and it was designed to provide pressure head of at\\r\\n288:least 20 m to all of its consumers. The normal operating pressure in\\r\\n289:the network ranges between 20 and 30 m. A pressure reduction\\r\\n290-valve (PRV) was installed at the lower part of the town (Area B)\\r\\n291-to help reduce background leakages. The network has different\\r\\n292:pressure areas, and therefore exhibits different sensitivity to leak-\\r\\n293-ages. PRVs were also installed downstream of the two main reser-\\r\\n294:voirs to help regulate the pressure. A pump and a water tank were\\r\\n295-installed in the higher part of the town (Area C) to provide suffi-\\r\\n296:cient pressure to the consumers of that area. The tank has a diameter\\r\\n297-of 16 m with a cylindrical shape. The pump was programmed so the\\r\\n298-tank refills during the night and empties to Area C during the day.\\r\\n299-The design decision to include pipes of 50 m length was based\\r\\n300-on the following considerations: First, it is common for a real net-\\r\\n301-work to have consumer demand locations at a 50-m interval; thus,\\r\\n--\\n338-where db\\r\\n339-i = base demand of node i; n = number of consumer types;\\r\\n340-αj\\r\\n341-i = percentage of the jth consumer type at the ith node; βj\\r\\n342-i =\\r\\n343:average amount of water consumed in m3=h for each m2 of a\\r\\n344-Fig. 1. L-Town benchmark network.\\r\\n345-© ASCE\\r\\n346-04022068-3\\r\\n347-J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\\r\\n348- J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(12): 04022068 \\r\\n--\\n352---- PDF PAGE 6 ---\\r\\n353-building; and γi = total building area corresponding to node i. In\\r\\n354-this benchmark, three types of consumers (n ¼ 3) were considered:\\r\\n355-residential, commercial, and industrial.\\r\\n356-Each node has a unique demand pattern for each consumer\\r\\n357:type based on the statistical characteristics of real metered data\\r\\n358-from the area. Specifically, a Fourier series model was used to\\r\\n359:approximate the demands (Vrachimis et al. 2018c), capturing sea-\\r\\n360-sonality (weekly, yearly) as well as the uncertainties on demand\\r\\n361-patterns (Fig. 2). The overall water consumption is the linear com-\\r\\n362-bination of the base demands with the corresponding patterns.\\r\\n363-The demand peaking factor, which is the ratio of the maximum\\r\\n364:daily demand (MDD) to the average daily demand (ADD) in a\\r\\n365-water system, was also considered in the design of demand pat-\\r\\n366-terns. The ratio, based on observations from real systems, typically\\r\\n367-ranges from 1.2 for very large water systems to 3.0 or even higher\\r\\n368-for specific small systems. The demand peaking factor in L-Town\\r\\n369:ranges between 1.5 and 2.0, given it is an average size system.\\r\\n370-BattLeDIM Challenge Scenario\\r\\n371-As part of the Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation\\r\\n372-Methods, all participating teams were given the following artificial\\r\\n373-scenario to establish the challenge:\\r\\n374-In previous years, the utility of L-Town was experiencing a\\r\\n375-large number of pipe breaks and water losses, affecting its\\r\\n376:service quality. During 2018, a number of leakage events oc-\\r\\n377-curred, which were detected and fixed by the water utility.\\r\\n378-However, it is believed that a number of smaller leakages oc-\\r\\n379-curred but not revealed. It is also assumed that some leakages\\r\\n380-occurred abruptly, whereas others developed gradually, as\\r\\n381-incipient events, from background leaks into pipe bursts.\\r\\n--\\n384-bution network, in which base demands were assigned to nodes,\\r\\n385-following historical billing data of proximity consumers. Moreover,\\r\\n386-two nominal demand patterns were identified for residential and\\r\\n387-commercial consumer types (with some discrepancies). The utility\\r\\n388-believes that there might be some inaccuracies in the model,\\r\\n389:e.g., with respect to the pipe roughness and pipe diameters.\\r\\n390-In addition, the utility was not able to confirm the status of all the\\r\\n391-valves in the network (i.e., whether they are open or closed).\\r\\n392-The L-Town water utility is searching for a solution to help\\r\\n393:them analyze the SCADA dataset, and detect leakage events as\\r\\n394-fast as possible. In addition, it is crucial for the utility to have\\r\\n395-an indication where approximately the leakage occurs, so that\\r\\n396-the field workers can inspect those potential leaks using their\\r\\n397-equipment.\\r\\n398-The L-Town utility has created an open call for teams to dem-\\r\\n399-onstrate their ability in detecting and localizing leakage events.\\r\\n400:The teams are given a historical SCADA dataset along with infor-\\r\\n401-mation related with the leakages detected and fixed by the utility\\r\\n402:throughout 2018, to use for training purposes and for calibrating\\r\\n403-their models. It is possible that more leakage events occurred\\r\\n404:during 2018, however the utility was not able to detect and local-\\r\\n405-ize them.\\r\\n406:Throughout 2019, the utility conducted periodic surveys using\\r\\n407-additional sensing equipment, pipe inspections and other methods,\\r\\n408-and was able to detect and isolate all the leakage e…13376 tokens truncated…ight\\r\\n837-Table 4. Attack detection algorithms ranked by overall ranking score (S), assessed in terms of number of attacks detected; time-to-detection (STTD);\\r\\n838-accuracy (SCLF); true positive ratio (TPR); true negative ratio (TNR); and number of true positives (TP), false positives (FP), true negatives (TN), and\\r\\n839-false negatives (FN)\\r\\n840-Rank\\r\\n--\\n954-attack detection problems, although their performance would\\r\\n955-probably vary with the modeling context at hand.\\r\\n956-•\\r\\n957-Only a few algorithms provided information on the attacked de-\\r\\n958-vices. Among these, the algorithms proposed by Brentan et al.\\r\\n959:(2017) and Giacomoni et al. (2017) were the most accurate.\\r\\n960-•\\r\\n961-Most teams presented multistage detection methods. Comparing\\r\\n962-and confirming the detection issued by different modules can\\r\\n963-help decrease classification errors.\\r\\n964-•\\r\\n965-Detection algorithms adopting a multivariate approach may be\\r\\n966-better suited than algorithms analyzing a single time series per\\r\\n967-time. The inherent interdependence of the elements in the water\\r\\n968-network should theoretically allow for the detection of anoma-\\r\\n969-lies even when adversaries try to conceal their actions by alter-\\r\\n970:ing the SCADA readings of one or a few deployed sensors. Such\\r\\n971-interdependence generally presents a nonlinear nature, which\\r\\n972-can be well described by nonlinear models such as those belong-\\r\\n973-ing to the class of artificial neural networks.\\r\\n974-•\\r\\n975-The adoption of supervised classification algorithms that learn\\r\\n--\\n979-should always be combined with cross-validation schemes.\\r\\n980-•\\r\\n981-It appears that consistency checks and the analysis of control\\r\\n982-rules should lead to the identification of the simplest attacks.\\r\\n983-The results described previously were obtained using three spe-\\r\\n984:cific data sets, which represent only a small portion of the entire set\\r\\n985-of cyber attacks that could threaten a water distribution system.\\r\\n986-Hence, the generation of different attacks is likely to produce differ-\\r\\n987-ent results—a limitation observed in other battles (e.g., Ostfeld\\r\\n988-et al. 2008).\\r\\n989-Another factor that influenced the BATADAL results relates to\\r\\n990-the evaluation criteria. First, the time-to-detection score STTD was\\r\\n991-based on the ratio between the time taken to detect an attack and the\\r\\n992-attack duration; this implies that a 2-h attack detected within 1 h\\r\\n993:would have the same score as a 10-h attack detected in Hour 5.\\r\\n994-Some operators may prefer to define scores that account explicitly\\r\\n995-for the absolute value of the attack duration or its corresponding\\r\\n996-damage. Second, the classification performance score SCLF is based\\r\\n997-on TPR and TNR, which are common metrics for classification\\r\\n998-problems. However, other metrics may be adopted, such as the\\r\\n--\\n1025-(c)\\r\\n1026-(d)\\r\\n1027-(e)\\r\\n1028-(f)\\r\\n1029-(g)\\r\\n1030:Fig. 4. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2018, 144(8): 04018048 \\r\\n1037-Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by University of Texas at San Antonio on 06/11/18. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\\r\\n1038-\\r\\n1039-\\r\\n1040---- PDF PAGE 10 ---\\r\\n1041-scenarios comprising varying hydraulic conditions (i.e., water\\r\\n1042:demand or initial tank levels) and multiple attack sequences.\\r\\n1043-•\\r\\n1044:Use of real SCADA data: A major limitation of the current re-\\r\\n1045-search in cybersecurity is the absence of detailed information\\r\\n1046-about cyber attacks on water utilities (e.g., timing, compromised\\r\\n1047-devices, or hydraulic response of the system). Access to such\\r\\n1048:information and to the corresponding SCADA data—perhaps\\r\\n1049-in some anonymized forms—would drastically enhance the un-\\r\\n1050-derstanding of the skills and limitations of detection algorithms.\\r\\n1051:Another challenge with SCADA data is that they often contain\\r\\n1052-noise and measurement errors, so attack detection algorithms\\r\\n1053-should be coupled with data preprocessing techniques.\\r\\n1054-•\\r\\n1055:Pressure deficient conditions and water quality problems: A lim-\\r\\n1056-itation of this battle is its reliance on data generated with a\\r\\n1057:demand-driven engine (Taormina et al. 2017). The range of at-\\r\\n1058:tacks should be thus extended to include pressure-deficient con-\\r\\n1059-ditions, water quality problems, and adversial attempts aimed at\\r\\n1060-threatening emergency responses, such as firefighting opera-\\r\\n1061:tions. In the absence of real SCADA data, simulated data could\\r\\n1062-be generated by combining epanetCPA with more-sophisticated\\r\\n1063-hydraulic engines (e.g., Sayyed et al. 2015) or water quality\\r\\n1064-models, e.g., EPANET-MSX (Shang et al. 2007).\\r\\n1065-•\\r\\n1066-Sensitivity analysis: The definition of the cutoff criteria defining\\r\\n1067-outliers regulates the trade-off between TPR and TNR for most\\r\\n1068-of the algorithms, so there is a need to adopt or develop sensi-\\r\\n1069-tivity analysis tools that draw the appropriate line between nor-\\r\\n1070:mal and anomalous data (Abokifa et al. 2017). This step should\\r\\n1071-always precede the application of an algorithm to new data\\r\\n1072:sets—or its deployment in a SCADA system.\\r\\n1073-•\\r\\n1074-Computational requirements and scalability to large networks:\\r\\n1075-The algorithms presented in this paper were applied to a\\r\\n1076-medium-sized water distribution system comprising one\\r\\n1077:SCADA system and nine PLCs. Because attack detection algo-\\r\\n1078-rithm are meant to run in real-time, it is necessary to evaluate\\r\\n1079-their computational requirements as well as their scalability to\\r\\n1080-larger networks.\\r\\n1081-•\\r\\n1082-Attack localization: To facilitate and hasten incident resolu-\\r\\n1083-tion, an ideal detection mechanism should be able to identify\\r\\n1084-which components of the network are being attacked. This is a\\r\\n1085-rather challenging task due to the intrinsic correlation among\\r\\n1086-the hydraulic variables. For data-driven detection mechanisms,\\r\\n1087-the task may be solved with variable (or feature) selection al-\\r\\n1088:gorithms (Galelli et al. 2014; Karakaya et al. 2016), which\\r\\n1089-identify the variables that are strongly related to the detected\\r\\n1090-anomalies.\\r\\n1091-•\\r\\n1092-Integration with other fault detection mechanisms: Because at-\\r\\n1093-tack detection mechanisms aim to disclose outliers and contex-\\r\\n1094-tual anomalies in the system behavior, they may accidentally\\r\\n1095-disclose anomalous behaviors that are not necessarily caused\\r\\n1096:by cyber attacks (e.g., a water level sensor reporting wrong read-\\r\\n1097-ings or a malfunctioning pump). Hence, there is a need to dis-\\r\\n1098-close the nature of each problem being identified—for example,\\r\\n1099-by combining the attack detection algorithms with fault detec-\\r\\n1100-tion mechanisms that monitor the operations of PLCs.\\r\\n1101-•\\r\\n--\\n1109-sponding cost savings guaranteed by a detection algorithm.\\r\\n1110-Closure\\r\\n1111-The BATADAL was the first battle competition dealing with the\\r\\n1112-emerging topic of cyber-physical security of water distribution sys-\\r\\n1113-tems. This battle provided an opportunity to develop, test, and com-\\r\\n1114:pare attack detection algorithms for SCADA data. The solutions\\r\\n1115-provided by seven teams suggest that timely and accurate detection\\r\\n1116-can be obtained by both model-based and data-driven approaches,\\r\\n1117-usually made of multiple sequential stages. Although the data and\\r\\n1118-algorithms presented here provide a first step toward an objective\\r\\n1119-comparison of attack detection algorithms for water distribution\\r\\n1120-systems, they do not represent the entire spectrum of modeling con-\\r\\n1121-texts that practitioners and researchers would encounter. Hence, the\\r\\n1122-authors hope that the availability of a dedicated website (BATADAL\\r\\n1123:2017) will help share more data sets and case studies.\\r\\n1124-Acknowledgments\\r\\n1125-Riccardo Taormina, Stefano Galelli, and Nils Ole Tippenhauer are\\r\\n1126-supported by the National Research Foundation (NRF), Singapore,\\r\\n1127-under its National Cybersecurity R&D Programme (Award No.\\r\\n1128-NRF2014NCR-NCR001-40). 400: The teams are given a historical SCADA dataset along with infor-
401: mation related with the leakages detected and fixed by the utility
402: throughout 2018, to use for training purposes and for calibrating
403: their models. It is possible that more leakage events occurred
404: during 2018, however the utility was not able to detect and local-
405: ize them.
406: Throughout 2019, the utility conducted periodic surveys using
407: additional sensing equipment, pipe inspections and other methods,
408: and was able to detect and isolate all the leakage events that oc-
409: curred within that period. The most critical of these events were
410: repaired, however it was not possible to repair some of these leak-
411: ages due to financial reasons.
412: The overall goal of this competition, is to identify methods
413: which are able to detect and localize the leakage events that oc-
414: curred in L-Town in 2019, as fast as possible (with respect to time)
415: and as accurately as possible (with respect to their location), in or-
416: der to minimize their overall financial costs, both in water losses, as
417: well as due to the hours spent in isolating the leakage by the utility
418: staff. The L-Town utility will compare the different solutions and
419: select the best one based on that objective.
420: Scenario Generation and Available Data

497: Simulation and Data Set Generation Engine
498: The data set generation engine takes as input a structured file data-
499: set_configuration.yalm, which includes the start and end time of the
500: simulation, the leakages (including the start and end time, the leak
501: diameter, the type of the leakage, and its peak time), and the loca-
502: tions of the sensors [flow, pressure, automated metered readings
503: (AMRs), and level sensors].
504: The hydraulic simulations were executed using WNTR, a
505: Python package that supports pressure-driven demand simulations
506: and leakage modeling (Klise et al. 2017). Specifically, for the
507: pressure-driven demands, we computed a new demand for the
508: ith node DiðkÞ using the function fPDD, such that DiðkÞ ¼
509: fPDDðpiðkÞ; diðkÞÞ, where piðkÞ is the pressure and diðkÞ is the re-
510: quested demand at node i: If the computed pressure is piðkÞ < P0
511: then the demand is zero, i.e., DiðkÞ ¼ 0. If the pressure is piðkÞ >
512: Pf, then the demand equals the requested demand, i.e., DiðkÞ ¼
513: diðkÞ. Finally, in the case where the pressure is P0 ≤piðkÞ ≤Pf,
514: then the demand is calculated as DiðkÞ ¼ diðkÞððpiðkÞ −P0Þ=
515: ðPf −P0ÞÞδ. In BattLeDIM, we consider the following parameters:
516: P0 ¼ 7, Pf ¼ 25, and δ ¼ 0.5. The values for Pf and δ are the
517: default values used in WNTR, while the minimum pressure value
518: P0 ¼ 7 was increased from 3.5 to 7 m because this minimum
519: value was never observed in the L-Town network during the con-
520: sidered scenarios.
521: Using the pressure-dependent demand simulation, the node de-
522: mand DiðkÞ starts to decrease compared to the requested demand
523: diðkÞ when the pressure is below Pf and goes to zero when pressure
524: is below P0.
525: Nominal and Real Models
526: In practice, it is difficult to have an accurate model of the real
527: system. For this reason, a nominal EPANET L-Town model
528: was provided to the BattLeDIM participants; however, a real
529: model (which was unknown to the competitors) was used to gen-
530: erate the SCADA data set. In general, the nominal model approx-
531: imates the real, with some uncertainties. The nominal model was
532: generated by randomizing parameters of the real L-Town network
533: using the EPANET-MATLAB Toolkit (Eliades et al. 2016), as
534: follows:
535: •
536: Base demands: Base demands for each consumer type at each
537: node are randomized uniformly between 10% compared to the
538: real value.
539: •
540: Demand patterns: Nominal residential and commercial patterns
541: are available; however, industrial patterns are not available. The
542: patterns used in the real model are unique for each node and may
543: differ significantly from the nominal patterns, while they also
544: include a significant noise component.
545: •
546: Pipe parameter uncertainty: All pipe parameters (roughness,
547: length, and diameter) are randomized uniformly between
548: 10% of their real value. This randomization aims to represent
549: the uncertainty of hydraulic resistance, which is a function of all
550: the aforementioned pipe parameters. In reality, parameter uncer-
551: tainties may have different magnitudes. Typically, the most
552: uncertain parameter is pipe roughness, while pipe length and
553: diameter are less uncertain.
554: •
555: Topological uncertainty: Two pipes (p37 and p251) were ran-
556: domly selected to be closed in the real network, whereas in
557: the nominal model they appeared to be open. The term topologi-
558: cal uncertainty is used here to describe the variability of the
559: topological graph of the network due to a pipe valve with un-
560: known status (open/closed). This can also be considered as op-
561: erational uncertainty because, typically, valves change status
562: during operations, such as repairs, that have taken place in
563: the network.
564: Sensors and Telemetry
565: We assume that there is one tank water level sensor, a total of three
566: flow sensors, one at the pump and one at each of the DMA entran-
567: ces, and 33 pressure sensors, all transmitting their measurements
568: every 5 min to the utility’s SCADA system. There are no time de-
569: lays in the data transmission and no lost packages. Pressure sensors
570: give an average value of the last 5 min, which mitigates the uncer-

222: Calibration Network (Ostfeld et al. 2012). The network consists
223: of 429 pipes, 388 junctions, 7 storage tanks, 11 pumps (distributed
224: across 5 pumping stations), 5 valves, and a single reservoir (Fig. 1).
225: Water consumption is fairly regular throughout the year. These
226: physical assets are augmented with a network of 9 PLCs, which
227: are located in proximity to pumps, storage tanks, and valves. Most
228: of the PLCs controlling the pumps receive the information needed
229: by the control logic from other PLCs—for instance, PLC1 controls
230: Pumps PU1 and PU2 on the basis of Tank T1 water level, which is
231: monitored by PLC2 (Table 1). PLCs controlling pumps and valves
232: record information on the device status (on/off or open/closed),
233: the flow passing through it, and the inlet and outlet pressure of
234: pumping stations. The cyber network includes a SCADA system,
235: which coordinates the operations and stores the readings provided
236: by the PLCs. All information regarding the distribution system
237: were incorporated into the EPANET2 (Rossman 2000) input file
238: C-Town.inp which was provided to the participants. Water demand
239: in all nodes of C-Town was not shared, meaning that participants
240: could not run the model for the same period and then compare the
241: results with the provided SCADA data.
242: Development Data
243: Participants were provided with three data sets containing SCADA
244: readings for 43 system variables, i.e., tank water levels (7 variables,

306: •
307: Training Data Set 1 was generated with a simulation horizon of
308: 365 days. A key aspect of the data set was the absence of cyber
309: attacks, which made it suitable for studying the operations of the
310: water distribution system under normal operating conditions.
311: •
312: Training Data Set 2 contained 7 attacks, spanning 492 hourly time
313: steps. One attack was entirely revealed to the participants (by
314: appropriately labelling the corresponding time steps), whereas
315: the remaining attacks were either partially revealed or hidden
316: (Table 2). This corresponds to a postattack scenario in which for-
317: ensics experts carry out an investigation to determine whether,
318: when, and where the water distribution system has been affected.
319: •
320: Test Data Set contained 7 additional attacks, spanning over
321: 407 hourly time steps (Table 3). Naturally, no information
322: regarding the attacks was revealed. Participants were required
323: to run the detection algorithms on Test Data Set and submit
324: a detection report containing the following information:
325: number of attacks detected, start and end time of each attack
326: (in DD-MM-YYYY HH format), and the label of the attacked
327: device(s) (optional).
328: The operations of the water system were altered through mali-
329: cious activation of hydraulic actuators, change of actuator settings,
330: and deception attacks—among the most common attacks on cyber-
331: physical systems (Cardenas et al. 2009). The latter were aimed at
332: manipulating the information sent or received by sensors and PLCs,
333: with the ultimate goal of affecting the operations of an actuator
334: (Urbina et al. 2016). Deception attacks were also used to alter the
335: information received by the SCADA system, therefore concealing
336: the real, physical outcomes of the attacks. SCADA concealment
337: was performed by either adding an offset to the transmitted sensor
338: readings or by replacing actual traffic information between the
339: PLCs and the SCADA system with previously recorded data, a type
340: of manipulation known as a replay attack (Urbina et al. 2016). The
341: replay attacks featured in the BATADAL consisted of replacing
342: data for a given hour of the day with those recorded during the same
343: hour 1 or 2 days previous. Fig. 2 illustrates Attack #3 (Training
344: Data Set 2), in which both pump operations and SCADA data were
345: compromised. In this case, a deception attack manipulated Tank T1
346: water level readings sent by PLC2–PLC1. PLC1 received a reading
347: of 0.5 m, which was below the low-level thresholds that activate
348: Pumps PU1 and PU2 (4 and 1 m, respectively). This resulted in
349: both pumps working for the entire period of the attack, which lasted
350: 60 h. Consequently, the water level in Tank T1 reached the full tank
351: level (6.5 m), with the excess water being spilled. The adversary
352: tried to conceal the surge in T1 water level with a second deception
353: attack that altered the signal sent by PLC2 to the SCADA system
354: with a time-varying offset.

481: Time-to-Detection
482: The time-to-detection (TTD) is the time needed by an algorithm to
483: disclose a threat. It is defined as the difference between the time td at
484: which the attack is detected and the time t0 at which the attack starts
485: TTD ¼ td −t0
486: ð1Þ
487: The value of td is inferred from the detection report, and it cor-
488: responds to the first time stamp flagged as under attack while the
489: attack is ongoing. The lower the value of TTD, the better the algo-
490: rithm performs. If an attack is detected
491: 0 ≤TTD ≤Δt
492: ð2Þ
493: where Δt = total duration of the attack. If the attack is not de-
494: tected while it is ongoing (or at all), TTD ¼ Δt. To facilitate the
495: comparison of all algorithms under different attack scenarios, the
496: following performance score (STTD) was computed:
497: STTD ¼ 1 −1
498: na
499: X
500: na

502: TTDi

505: where na = number of attacks contained in a data set; TTDi =
506: time-to-detection of the ith attack; and Δti = corresponding dura-
507: tion. The value of STTD varies between 0 and 1, with STTD ¼ 1 being
508: the ideal case in which all attacks are immediately detected and
509: STTD ¼ 0 being the case in which none of the attacks are detected.

from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor

ROOT = pathlib.Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
OUT = ROOT / 'build' / 'audit_o_evidence'
OUT.mkdir(exist_ok=True)

 'zenodo_record.json': 'https://zenodo.org/api/records/4017659',
 'official_2018_Leakages.csv': 'https://zenodo.org/records/4017659/files/2018_Leakages.csv?download=1',
 'official_2019_Leakages.csv': 'https://zenodo.org/records/4017659/files/2019_Leakages.csv?download=1',

 urls[key+'.json']='https://api.crossref.org/works/'+urllib.parse.quote(doi,safe='')

def fetch(pair):
 name,url=pair
 try:
  req=urllib.request.Request(url,headers={'User-Agent':'Mozilla/5.0 academic dataset verification'})
  with urllib.request.urlopen(req,timeout=45) as response:
   content=response.read()
   result={'url':url,'status':response.status,'bytes':len(content),'sha256':hashlib.sha256(content).hexdigest()}
  (OUT/name).write_bytes(content)
  return name,result
 except Exception as exc:return name,{'url':url,'error':str(exc)}

results=dict(ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=5).map(fetch,urls.items()))
print('DOWNLOADS',json.dumps(results),flush=True)

def audit_csv(path,delimiter):
 with path.open(encoding='utf-8-sig',newline='') as f:
  reader=csv.reader(f,delimiter=delimiter);header=[s.strip() for s in next(reader)]
  n=0;labels={};first=last=None
  for row in reader:
   if not row:continue
   n+=1
   if first is None:first=row[0]
   last=row[0]
   if 'ATT_FLAG' in header:
    v=row[header.index('ATT_FLAG')].strip();labels[v]=labels.get(v,0)+1
 return {'rows':n,'header':header,'first':first,'last':last,'labels':labels,'md5':hashlib.md5(path.read_bytes()).hexdigest()}

audits={}
local=ROOT.parent/'research'/'data'
for p in (local/'water_battledim'/'BattLeDIM').glob('*.csv'):audits['kaggle/'+p.name]=audit_csv(p,';')
for p in (local/'water_batadal').glob('*.csv'):audits['kaggle/'+p.name]=audit_csv(p,',')
for p in OUT.glob('*.csv'):audits['official/'+p.name]=audit_csv(p,';' if 'Leakages' in p.name else ',')
zip_path=OUT/'official_batadal_test.zip'
if zip_path.exists():
 with zipfile.ZipFile(zip_path) as z:
  for name in z.namelist():
   if name.lower().endswith('.csv'):
    p=OUT/('official_test_'+pathlib.PurePosixPath(name).name);p.write_bytes(z.read(name))
    audits['official/'+p.name]=audit_csv(p,',')

record=OUT/'zenodo_record.json'
matches={}
if record.exists():

 for f in record['files']:

  if key in audits:matches[f['key']]={'official':f['checksum'],'local':'md5:'+audits[key]['md5'],'match':f['checksum']=='md5:'+audits[key]['md5']}

paper_metadata={}
for p in OUT.glob('paper_*.json'):
 m=json.loads(p.read_text())['message']
 paper_metadata[p.stem]={k:m.get(k) for k in ['DOI','title','author','published','published-online','type','container-title','volume','issue','page']}
result={'checked_at':datetime.datetime.now(datetime.timezone.utc).isoformat(),'downloads':results,'csv':audits,'zenodo_checksums':matches,'papers':paper_metadata}
(OUT/'audit_results.json').write_text(json.dumps(result,indent=2),encoding='utf-8')
print('COUNTS',json.dumps({k:{'rows':v['rows'],'columns':len(v['header']),'labels':v['labels']} for k,v in audits.items()}),flush=True)
print('ZENODO_MATCHES',json.dumps(matches),flush=True)
print('PAPERS',json.dumps(paper_metadata),flush=True)

Note. This manuscript was submitted on August 21, 2025; approved on 
March 19, 2026; published online on June 3, 2026. Discussion period open 
until November 3, 2026; separate discussions must be submitted for individual

Dynamic Pressure Regulation in Water Distribution 
Systems through Remote Critical Node Control: 
A Case Study
A. Arango-Martinez1; E. Rosero-García2; and E. Franco-Mejia, Ph.D.3
Abstract: This paper presents a field-oriented methodology for modeling and controlling pressure regulation, demonstrated on a real water 
distribution network (WDN) sector. The aim is to provide a practical and reproducible pathway from field measurements to deployable 
real-time pressure control, addressing the gap between simulator-based numerical studies and full-scale field implementations. The study 
covers data-driven dynamic modeling, controller design, and pre-deployment performance assessment using real operational data. A low-order 
linear dynamic model with transport delay is identified directly from field measurements, combining 20 days of pressure and inflow data with 
step-response tests performed on an existing pressure-reducing valve (PRV). The model is validated against an independent 24-h dataset of real 
consumption, achieving a maximum deviation of approximately ±1 m H2O at the remote critical node, which is adequate for control-oriented 
modeling and controller synthesis. Based on the identified model, a digital reference signal tracking (RST) controller with a Smith predictor is 
designed using explicit performance specifications for settling time, overshoot, and bandwidth. The controller is evaluated in silico using real 
consumption data not used during identification, providing an unbiased assessment of closed-loop performance under representative demand 
variability. Results show that the proposed strategy maintains pressure at the remote critical node within acceptable bounds while effectively 
rejecting demand-induced disturbances. Although closed-loop field implementation is beyond the scope of this study, the framework is explicitly 
conceived as a preparatory step for real-time deployment. The main conclusion is that a transparent, data-driven methodology grounded in field 
measurements can reduce modeling and tuning effort while yielding controller designs compatible with standard programmable logic controller 
(PLC) and supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) platforms, enabling utilities to progress systematically toward practical real-time 
pressure control. DOI: 10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-7321. © 2026 American Society of Civil Engineers.
Practical Applications: This study offers practical guidance for water utilities seeking to improve pressure management while reducing water 
losses and infrastructure wear. Using real operational data, the proposed approach shows how effective pressure regulation strategies can be 
derived directly from field measurements and implemented with standard PLC and SCADA systems already used by most utilities. As a result, 
the methodology is accessible without requiring advanced hydraulic-modeling expertise or specialized simulation tools. Pressure management 
is particularly important because daily demand variations often produce excessive pressures during low consumption periods (e.g., nighttime). 
These pressure surges increase the likelihood of pipe bursts, background leakage, and premature deterioration of network assets. The proposed 
control strategy addresses this issue by stabilizing pressure at a critical point in the network, allowing utilities to maintain pressures within 
acceptable and regulatory limits despite changing demand. While the controller has not yet been deployed in closed-loop field operation, the 
methodology is designed as a practical step toward real-time implementation. By reducing modeling effort and simplifying controller tuning, the 
approach lowers technical barriers for utilities interested in advanced pressure control and provides a realistic foundation for improving network 
reliability and reducing operational costs.
Introduction
Water utilities must deliver potable water at adequate flow and 
pressure while minimizing water losses, operational costs, and 
asset deterioration. Modern water distribution networks (WDNs) 
exhibit pronounced intraday and seasonal demand variability, lead-
ing to large fluctuations in service pressure (Bello et al. 2019; Fan 
et al. 2017). When pressures exceed acceptable limits—particularly 
during low-demand periods—background leakage increases, pipe 
failure risk rises, and infrastructure lifetime is reduced (Van Zyl 
et al. 2008; Page 2018). Stabilizing and reducing pressure is there-
fore among the most effective strategies to mitigate leakage and 
bursts, with substantial long-term economic benefits (Vicente et al. 
2016; Creaco and Walski 2017).
In practice, pressure management typically relies on pressure-
reducing valves (PRVs) operated under relatively simple control 
schemes (Bello et al. 2019; Campisano et al. 2010; Creaco et al. 
2019; Galuppini et al. 2019). A common solution is the mechan‐
ically piloted PRV with a fixed downstream setpoint (Doghri 
et al. 2022; Ferrarese and Malavasi 2022). More advanced 
electronic add-on controllers modulate outlet pressure using 
predefined day–night profiles (time-based control) or as a func‐
tion of inlet flow (flow-modulated control) to approximately

nal tracking (RST) controller with explicit performance criteria, and 
test it off-line with real data prior to deployment.                           
Contributions and Gap Addressed
This paper bridges advanced RTC concepts and practical deploy-
ment by providing a transparent, data-driven, repeatable methodol-
ogy for modeling and controlling pressure at a remote critical node 
in a real WDN sector. The main contributions are:
1. Data-driven dynamic modeling of a PRV–network sector. 
Using field measurements, we identify a low-order linear 
dynamic model with transport delay linking PRV pilot actuation 
to remote-node pressure. Static nonlinear demand–headloss 
behavior is separated from the dynamic response, yielding a 
controller-oriented representation.
2. Discrete-time RST controller design with Smith predictor. 
Based on the identified model, we design a digital RST con-
troller with explicit delay compensation, enabling systematic 
specification of closed-loop dynamics, disturbance rejection, 
and tracking performance.
3. Validation with real consumption data. The controller is tested 
in silico using an independent 24-h dataset of real consumption 
patterns, demonstrating accurate pressure regulation and robust-
ness to demand variability.
4. Implementation readiness. The workflow is designed for 
implementation on standard PLC/SCADA systems using exist-
ing telemetry and PRV hardware, without specialized devices or 
hydraulic solvers.
A “Limitations and Future Work” subsection clarifies that the 
controller is validated only in silico and that field deployment is the 
natural next step.
The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. The sub-
sequent section details the modeling process for remote-node pres-
sure, including the hydraulic sector description, instrumentation, 
model identification, disturbance modeling, and validation. The 
next section presents the controller design and the closed-loop 
evaluation under real demand data. Finally, the paper summarizes 
the main conclusions and outlines future work.
Modeling of Remote-Node Pressure
Designing a remote-node pressure regulation system requires a 
model that relates remote-node pressure dynamics to PRV actuation 
and variations in consumer demand. This section describes the 
hydraulic sector, instrumentation, and the identification procedure 
for the disturbance and dynamic models.
Hydraulic Sector
The study sector is located in Palmira (Valle del Cauca), Colombia. 
It is predominantly residential, with approximately 7,000 subscrib-
ers and an area of about 1 km2. The elevation difference between the 
lowest and highest points is approximately 14 m. The remote node 
(the highest point) lies approximately 3 m above the PRV location. 
The sector and elevation contours are shown in Fig. 1. The node 
labeled hm is the remote pressure-sensing point; “On duty” denotes 
the pressure-reducing valve.
The sector is supplied through a single inlet main equipped with 
two pilot-operated diaphragm PRVs installed in parallel on an 8-in. 
pipeline (see Fig. 1). Under normal operation, one PRV is open and 
controls downstream pressure, while the second remains closed as a 
standby valve to ensure redundancy. All experiments reported here 
were carried out on the duty PRV with the standby valve closed.
Instrumentation of the Hydraulic Sector
The duty PRV is equipped with a mechanical spring–diaphragm 
pressure-reducing pilot (Singer model 160) with a nominal outlet-
pressure adjustment range of 20–200 psi. The valve is not fitted 
with an electronic actuator; therefore, the outlet-pressure setpoint 
was adjusted manually by turning the pilot screw during tests. This 
configuration reflects a conventional hydraulic setup and allows 
assessment of sector dynamics without electronic actuation.
The sector is instrumented with an inlet flow sensor (qin), a 
pressure-head sensor upstream of the PRVs (hu), a pressure-head 
sensor downstream of the PRVs (hd), and a pressure-head sensor at 
the remote node (hm).
Throughout this paper, hu, hd, and hm denote piezometric pres-
sure head (m H2O) at the corresponding locations. For a node at 
elevation z, the hydraulic head is h = z + p/γ, where p is gauge 
pressure and γ is the specific weight of water. Because sensor ele-
vations are fixed and flow velocities are low, variations in measured 
head are equivalent to variations in nodal pressure. Accordingly, we 
use pressure as shorthand for pressure head. The disturbance model 
introduced in Eq. (2) uses the head loss, hl, between the PRV outlet 
and the remote node, which includes elevation and frictional losses.
Sensor data are transmitted to and stored in a data logger. During 
transient tests, the sampling rate was 1 Hz with a resolution of 0.5 m 
H2O. Data are transferred to the SCADA system for analysis.
Model Identification Process
The sector is represented by the block diagram in Fig. 2. Controller 
synthesis requires (1) a dynamic model linking the normalized pilot 
downstream setpoint, xp(t), to the remote-node pressure head, hm(t); 
and (2) a disturbance model describing average head loss between 
the PRV outlet and the remote node as a function of demand.
WDNs are inherently nonlinear, largely because friction losses 
scale approximately with the square of flow and because of complex

Robustness Assessment Under Identified Uncertainty
Robustness is quantified for the implemented architecture in Fig. 11 
(RST controller plus Smith predictor), using the complete closed-
loop path from hm, ref to hm. In this analysis, the Smith internal 
model is kept fixed at the nominal model, while process uncertainty 
is introduced as multiplicative gain variation in the real plant. The 
robustness interpretation follows standard feedback analysis based 
on sensitivity and complementary sensitivity functions (Skogestad 
and Postlethwaite 2005; Åström and Murray 2021).
The nominal process model used by the Smith predictor is
                       P0(z−1) = z−nGp(z−1),
n = 5                       (16)
and the uncertain real process is                                                      
                           PKm(z−1) = Kmz−nGp(z−1)                           (17)
For the structure in Fig. 11, the closed-loop transfer function 
from internal reference hm, ref to remote-node head hm is
 Tfull, Km(z−1) =
PKm(z−1)Tc(z−1)
Sc(z−1) + Rc(z−1)(PKm(z−1) + Gp(z−1) −P0(z−1))
                                                                                              (18)
In Fig. 11, the external reference href is mapped to the internal 
reference by
                       hm, ref(z−1) = GT(z−1)href(z−1)                       (19)
Therefore, the complete path from external reference to remote-
node pressure explicitly includes GT
                      hm(z−1)
href(z−1) = GT(z−1)Tfull, Km(z−1)                      (20)
Accordingly, the full closed-loop sensitivity pair is defined as
                   Sfull, Km(z−1) = 1 −Tfull, Km(z−1)

Hence, GT is part of the complete path href →hm [Eq. (20)], 
while the sensitivity analysis in Eq. (21) is intentionally formulated 
on the internal feedback path hm, ref →hm. Because GT acts as 
a stable prefilter in the reference channel, it does not modify 
the closed-loop characteristic equation [denominator in Eq. (18)]; 
therefore, feedback robustness margins are assessed on the inter-
nal loop.
Uncertainty was quantified from aligned field step tests in 
Fig. 6, using event-wise estimates Km = Δhm/Δxp and response-
onset delay estimates. For N = 10 events, the identified gain 
statistics are Km = 0.9626, σKm = 0.1400, and percentile bounds 
[Km, 5, Km, 95] = [0.7519, 1.2628]. Delay statistics are Td = 11.0 s, 
σTd = 4.19 s, with observed range [1,15] s. Based on these data, 
the gain-uncertainty set is Km ∈{0.75, 1.00, 1.26}, while controller 
polynomials and Smith internal model are kept fixed.
Fig. 15 reports |Sfull, Km(ejωTs)| and |Tfull, Km(ejωTs)| for 
Km ∈{0.75, 1.00, 1.26}. The computed peaks are Ms = {1.385,
1.574, 1.846} and Mt = {0.999, 0.999, 1.034}, with worst case at 
Km = 1.26. Thus, increasing process gain reduces robustness reserve, 
while full closed-loop responses remain bounded over the identified 
interval.                                                                                             
Fig. 16 shows the frequency response of Tfull, Km for the same 
uncertainty set. The −3 dB crossing frequencies are ω−3dB =
{0.476, 0.787, 1.088} rad/s for Km = {0.75, 1.00, 1.26}. The corre-
sponding phase-at-bandwidth indicator is PM−3 dB = {82.97°,
48.83°, 1.15°}. In addition, using the −180° phase crossing of 
Tfull, Km, the distance to 0 dB gives GMTfull = {10.41, 6.65,
3.12} dB. Both indicators decrease monotonically as Km increases, 
consistently with the reduction in robustness reserve observed in 
Fig. 15.                                                                                             
To connect the frequency-domain trends in Figs. 15 and 16 with 
time-domain behavior, Fig. 17 reports a focused closed-loop window 
using the Smith–RST configuration in Fig. 11, with fixed controller 
and nominal internal model. The plot includes the identified uncer‐
tainty cases (Km = {0.75, 1.00, 1.26}) and one additional stress-test 
case (Km = 2.00), outside the identified interval. The upper panel 
is referenced to hm, ref = 15 m H2O. Consistently, the lower panel 
reports the commanded downstream head on an absolute scale as 
hd, sp = U(z) + (15 −r) + hl, nom, where U(z) is 1:1 with the normal‐
ized pilot setpoint and hl, nom is obtained from Eq. (3) at the mean his‐
torical inflow (qin, nom = 23.90 L/s), giving hl, nom = 4.66 m H2O. 
The peaks around τ ≈10 s and τ ≈60 s are produced by imposed 
disturbance steps in the equivalent head-loss channel; physically, they 
represent changes in hl driven by inlet-flow variations qin through 
Eq. (3). For the identified set and for Km = 2.00, the controller remains 
bounded and rejects perturbations without steady-state offset, although 
the stress-test case shows larger transient excursions and control effort 
than the nominal case.
Conclusions
This paper presented a complete data-driven methodology for 
pressure regulation in WDNs, from field measurements on a real 
sector to the design of a digital RST controller with Smith predictor. 
A low-order LTI model with delay for the hydraulic path between a 
PRV and a remote critical node was identified from operational data 
and step tests and validated against an independent 24-h consump-
tion profile, with errors of about ±1 m H2O at the critical node. The 
controller was then validated in silico using real consumption data 
not used during identification, providing an unbiased prediction of 
closed-loop performance prior to field deployment.
Simulation results under real demand show that the controller 
rejects demand disturbances and tracks reference changes with

Fig. 17. Closed-loop responses for identified and stress-test gains.
zero steady-state error. A formal robustness assessment was addi-
tionally performed for the Smith–RST architecture using the full 
closed-loop path from hm, ref to hm. Over the tested uncertainty set 
Km ∈{0.75, 1.00, 1.26}, the worst sensitivity peaks are Ms = 1.846
and Mt = 1.034 (Fig. 15). The Bode analysis of Tfull shows a 
monotonic reduction of the frequency-domain reserves as gain 
increases, with PM−3dB decreasing from 82.97° to 1.15° and 
GMTfull from 10.41 to 3.12 dB (Fig. 16). Time-domain responses 
within the identified set remain stable and non-oscillatory, with 
preserved disturbance rejection (Fig. 17); the additional stress-test 
case Km = 2.00 (outside the identified set) remains stable but 
exhibits larger transient deviations and control effort, consistent 
with reduced robustness reserve under stronger gain mismatch.
This robustness result is model-based and limited to the identi-
fied uncertainty set; therefore, it should be interpreted as a rigorous 
verification of the implemented design, not as a full robust-control 
synthesis (e.g., H∞).
Beyond this case study, the main contribution is methodological. 
By combining a static demand–headloss model and a low-order 
dynamic model identified from field data with an RST–Smith 
predictor controller, this paper provides a transparent, replicable 
framework that can be applied to other DMAs using standard instru-
mentation and control hardware. In doing so, it helps bridge the gap 
between simulator-oriented RTC studies, proprietary commercial 
solutions, and practical model-based procedures that expose PRV–
network dynamics and closed-loop performance.
Limitations and Future Work
The present study is limited to in silico validation of the control-
ler using real consumption profiles, without yet deploying the 
algorithm on the utility’s SCADA/PLC infrastructure. A full field 
implementation is the next step and will be the subject of future 
work. This will allow assessing additional performance indica-
tors such as leakage reduction, energy savings, and operational 
robustness, thereby complementing existing field case studies and 
providing further evidence on the effectiveness of the proposed 
methodology. Future work will focus on field implementation of the 
proposed methodology in operational DMAs, which is a necessary 
step to fully bridge the gap between conceptual RTC frameworks 
and routine use in water utilities.
Data Availability Statement
Some or all data, models, or code that support the findings of this 
study are available from the corresponding author upon reasona-
ble request.

# File contents description:
"L-TOWN.inp" EPANET Model (INP): 
A model of the network is provided with nominal parameters for all the system elements. 
The nominal base demands for each node are based on average historical metered consumption. 
In general, the difference between the actual and the nominal values for each consumer type (residential and commercial)is less than 10%. 
Weekly demand profiles for two consumer types(residential and commercial) are also provided, however they do not capture the yearly seasonality. Furthermore, the EPANET model parameters may be different from the actual network parameters (e.g., diameters, roughness coefficients), and it is assumed than in general this difference is no greater than 10% of the nominal values.

"L-TOWN_Real.inp" EPANET Model (INP): 
The model of the network used to generate the datasets. 
Contains the real network parameters and consumer demands.
It does not contain any leakages.

Franco-Mejia, Ph.D.3\\r\\nAbstract: This paper presents a field-oriented methodology for modeling and controlling pressure regulation, demonstrated on a real water \\r\\ndistribution network (WDN) sector. The aim is to provide a practical and reproducible pathway from field measurements to deployable \\r\\nreal-time pressure control, addressing the gap between simulator-based numerical studies and full-scale field implementations. The study \\r\\ncovers data-driven dynamic modeling, controller design, and pre-deployment performance assessment using real operational data. A low-order \\r\\nlinear dynamic model with transport delay is identified directly from field measurements, combining 20 days of pressure and inflow data with \\r\\nstep-response tests performed on an existing pressure-reducing valve (PRV). The model is validated against an independent 24-h dataset of real \\r\\nconsumption, achieving a maximum deviation of approximately ±1 m H2O at the remote critical node, which is adequate for control-oriented \\r\\nmodeling and controller synthesis. Based on the identified model, a digital reference signal tracking (RST) controller with a Smith predictor is \\r\\ndesigned using explicit performance specifications for settling time, overshoot, and bandwidth. The controller is evaluated in silico using real \\r\\nconsumption data not used during identification, providing an unbiased assessment of closed-loop performance under representative demand \\r\\nvariability. Results show that the proposed strategy maintains pressure at the remote critical node within acceptable bounds while effectively \\r\\nrejecting demand-induced disturbances. Although closed-loop field implementation is beyond the scope of this study, the framework is explicitly \\r\\nconceived as a preparatory step for real-time deployment. The main conclusion is that a transparent, data-driven methodology grounded in field \\r\\nmeasurements can reduce modeling and tuning effort while yielding controller designs compatible with standard programmable logic controller \\r\\n(PLC) and supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) platforms, enabling utilities to progress systematically toward practical real-time \\r\\npressure control. DOI: 10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-7321. © 2026 American Society of Civil Engineers.\\r\\nPractical Applications: This study offers practical guidance for water utilities seeking to improve pressure management while reducing water \\r\\nlosses and infrastructure wear. Using real operational data, the proposed approach shows how effective pressure regulation strategies can be \\r\\nderived directly from field measurements and implemented with standard PLC and SCADA systems already used by most utilities. As a result, \\r\\nthe methodology is accessible without requiring advanced hydraulic-modeling expertise or specialized simulation tools. Pressure management \\r\\nis particularly important because daily demand variations often produce excessive pressures during low consumption periods (e.g., nighttime). \\r\\nThese pressure surges increase the likelihood of pipe bursts, background leakage, and premature deterioration of network assets. The proposed \\r\\ncontrol strategy addresses this issue by stabilizing pressure at a critical point in the network, allowing utilities to maintain pressures within \\r\\nacceptable and regulatory limits despite changing demand. While the controller has not yet been deployed in closed-loop field operation, the \\r\\nmethodology is designed as a practical step toward real-time implementation. By reducing modeling effort and simplifying controller tuning, the \\r\\napproach lowers technical barriers for utilities interested in advanced pressure control and provides a realistic foundation for improving network \\r\\nreliability and reducing operational costs.\\r\\nIntroduction\\r\\nWater utilities must deliver potable water at adequate flow and \\r\\npressure while minimizing water losses, operational costs, and \\r\\nasset deterioration. Modern water distribution networks (WDNs) \\r\\nexhibit pronounced intraday and seasonal demand variability, lead-\\r\\ning to large fluctuations in service pressure (Bello et al. 2019; Fan \\r\\net al. 2017). When pressures exceed acceptable limits—particularly \\r\\nduring low-demand periods—background leakage increases, pipe \\r\\nfailure risk rises, and infrastructure lifetime is reduced (Van Zyl \\r\\net al. 2008; Page 2018). Stabilizing and reducing pressure is there-\\r\\nfore among the most effective strategies to mitigate leakage and \\r\\nbursts, with substantial long-term economic benefits (Vicente et al. \\r\\n2016; Creaco and Walski 2017).\\r\\nIn practice, pressure management typically relies on pressure-\\r\\nreducing valves (PRVs) operated under relatively simple control \\r\\nschemes (Bello et al. 2019; Campisano et al. 2010; Creaco et al. \\r\\n2019; Galuppini et al. 2019). A common solution is the mechan‐\\r\\nically piloted PRV with a fixed downstream setpoint (Doghri \\r\\net al. 2022; Ferrarese and Malavasi 2022). More advanced \\r\\nelectronic add-on controllers modulate outlet pressure using \\r\\npredefined day–night profiles (time-based control) or as a func‐\\r\\ntion of inlet flow (flow-modulated control) to approximately \\r\\n05026021-1\\r\\n© ASCE\\r\\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\\r\\nCase Study\\r\\n J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2026, 152(8): 05026021 \\r\\n Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by New Jersey Institute of Technology on 09/20/26. \\r\\nCopyright ASCE; all rights reserved, including rights for text and data mining and training of artificial intelligence or similar technologies. \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nUSER PAPER\\r\\n3 ---\\r\\nFig. 1. Hydraulic sector and elevation contours.\\r\\nour contribution is a systematic workflow that enables practitioners \\r\\nto identify an accurate reduced-order model, design a reference sig-\\r\\nnal tracking (RST) controller with explicit performance criteria, and \\r\\ntest it off-line with real data prior to deployment.                           \\r\\nContributions and Gap Addressed\\r\\nThis paper bridges advanced RTC concepts and practical deploy-\\r\\nment by providing a transparent, data-driven, repeatable methodol-\\r\\nogy for modeling and controlling pressure at a remote critical node \\r\\nin a real WDN sector. The main contributions are:\\r\\n1. Data-driven dynamic modeling of a PRV–network sector. \\r\\nUsing field measurements, we identify a low-order linear \\r\\ndynamic model with transport delay linking PRV pilot actuation \\r\\nto remote-node pressure. Static nonlinear demand–headloss \\r\\nbehavior is separated from the dynamic response, yielding a \\r\\ncontroller-oriented representation.\\r\\n2. Discrete-time RST controller design with Smith predictor. \\r\\nBased on the identified model, we design a digital RST con-\\r\\ntroller with explicit delay compensation, enabling systematic \\r\\nspecification of closed-loop dynamics, disturbance rejection, \\r\\nand tracking performance.\\r\\n3. Validation with real consumption data. The controller is tested \\r\\nin silico using an independent 24-h dataset of real consumption \\r\\npatterns, demonstrating accurate pressure regulation and robust-\\r\\nness to demand variability.\\r\\n4. Implementation readiness. The workflow is designed for \\r\\nimplementation on standard PLC/SCADA systems using exist-\\r\\ning telemetry and PRV hardware, without specialized devices or \\r\\nhydraulic solvers.\\r\\nA “Limitations and Future Work” subsection clarifies that the \\r\\ncontroller is validated only in silico and that field deployment is the \\r\\nnatural next step.\\r\\nThe remainder of this paper is organized as follows. The sub-\\r\\nsequent section details the modeling process for remote-node pres-\\r\\nsure, including the hydraulic sector description, instrumentation, \\r\\nmodel identification, disturbance modeling, and validation. The \\r\\nnext section presents the controller design and the closed-loop \\r\\nevaluation under real demand data. Finally, the paper summarizes \\r\\nthe main conclusions and outlines future work.\\r\\nModeling of Remote-Node Pressure\\r\\nDesigning a remote-node pressure regulation system requires a \\r\\nmodel that relates remote-node pressure dynamics to PRV actuation \\r\\nand variations in consumer demand. This section describes the \\r\\nhydraulic sector, instrumentation, and the identification procedure \\r\\nfor the disturbance and dynamic models.\\r\\nHydraulic Sector\\r\\nThe study sector is located in Palmira (Valle del Cauca), Colombia. \\r\\nIt is predominantly residential, with approximately 7,000 subscrib-\\r\\ners and an area of about 1 km2. The elevation difference between the \\r\\nlowest and highest points is approximately 14 m. The remote node \\r\\n(the highest point) lies approximately 3 m above the PRV location. \\r\\nThe sector and elevation contours are shown in Fig. 1. The node \\r\\nlabeled hm is the remote pressure-sensing point; “On duty” denotes \\r\\nthe pressure-reducing valve.\\r\\nThe sector is supplied through a single inlet main equipped with \\r\\ntwo pilot-operated diaphragm PRVs installed in parallel on an 8-in. \\r\\npipeline (see Fig. 1). Under normal operation, one PRV is open and \\r\\ncontrols downstream pressure, while the second remains closed as a \\r\\nstandby valve to ensure redundancy. All experiments reported here \\r\\nwere carried out on the duty PRV with the standby valve closed.\\r\\nInstrumentation of the Hydraulic Sector\\r\\nThe duty PRV is equipped with a mechanical spring–diaphragm \\r\\npressure-reducing pilot (Singer model 160) with a nominal outlet-\\r\\npressure adjustment range of 20–200 psi. The valve is not fitted \\r\\nwith an electronic actuator; therefore, the outlet-pressure setpoint \\r\\nwas adjusted manually by turning the pilot screw during tests. This \\r\\nconfiguration reflects a conventional hydraulic setup and allows \\r\\nassessment of sector dynamics without electronic actuation.\\r\\nThe sector is instrumented with an inlet flow sensor (qin), a \\r\\npressure-head sensor upstream of the PRVs (hu), a pressure-head \\r\\nsensor downstream of the PRVs (hd), and a pressure-head sensor at \\r\\nthe remote node (hm).\\r\\nThroughout this paper, hu, hd, and hm denote piezometric pres-\\r\\nsure head (m H2O) at the corresponding locations. For a node at \\r\\nelevation z, the hydraulic head is h = z + p/γ, where p is gauge \\r\\npressure and γ is the specific weight of water. Because sensor ele-\\r\\nvations are fixed and flow velocities are low, variations in measured \\r\\nhead are equivalent to variations in nodal pressure. Accordingly, we \\r\\nuse pressure as shorthand for pressure head. The disturbance model \\r\\nintroduced in Eq. (2) uses the head loss, hl, between the PRV outlet \\r\\nand the remote node, which includes elevation and frictional losses.\\r\\nSensor data are transmitted to and stored in a data logger. During \\r\\ntransient tests, the sampling rate was 1 Hz with a resolution of 0.5 m \\r\\nH2O. Data are transferred to the SCADA system for analysis.\\r\\nModel Identification Process\\r\\nThe sector is represented by the block diagram in Fig. 2. Controller \\r\\nsynthesis requires (1) a dynamic model linking the normalized pilot \\r\\ndownstream setpoint, xp(t), to the remote-node pressure head, hm(t); \\r\\nand (2) a disturbance model describing average head loss between \\r\\nthe PRV outlet and the remote node as a function of demand.\\r\\nWDNs are inherently nonlinear, largely because friction losses \\r\\nscale approximately with the square of flow and because of complex \\r\\n05026021-3\\r\\n© ASCE\\r\\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\\r\\n J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2026, 152(8): 05026021 \\r\\n Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by New Jersey Institute of Technology on 09/20/26. \\r\\nCopyright ASCE; all rights reserved, including rights for text and data mining and training of artificial intelligence or similar technologies. \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nUSER PAPER\\r\\n10 ---\\r\\nin Fig. 4. The demand-driven disturbances applied to the model \\r\\nare derived from measured inlet-flow data, preserving the diurnal \\r\\nvariability and stochastic characteristics observed during routine \\r\\noperation. The top subplot uses a single vertical scale for the \\r\\nactuator trajectory. Under the normalization defined in Eq. (1), U(z)\\r\\nand the equivalent pilot downstream setpoint hd, sp(t) have the same \\r\\nnumerical value over the adopted 0–100 m H2O range; therefore, \\r\\nthe same trace can be read either as the normalized command or \\r\\nas the corresponding pilot setpoint. The bottom subplot reports the \\r\\nresulting remote-node pressure head hm(t).\\r\\nRobustness Assessment Under Identified Uncertainty\\r\\nRobustness is quantified for the implemented architecture in Fig. 11 \\r\\n(RST controller plus Smith predictor), using the complete closed-\\r\\nloop path from hm, ref to hm. In this analysis, the Smith internal \\r\\nmodel is kept fixed at the nominal model, while process uncertainty \\r\\nis introduced as multiplicative gain variation in the real plant. The \\r\\nrobustness interpretation follows standard feedback analysis based \\r\\non sensitivity and complementary sensitivity functions (Skogestad \\r\\nand Postlethwaite 2005; Åström and Murray 2021).\\r\\nThe nominal process model used by the Smith predictor is\\r\\n                       P0(z−1) = z−nGp(z−1),\\r\\nn = 5                       (16)\\r\\nand the uncertain real process is                                                      \\r\\n                           PKm(z−1) = Kmz−nGp(z−1)                           (17)\\r\\nFor the structure in Fig. 11, the closed-loop transfer function \\r\\nfrom internal reference hm, ref to remote-node head hm is\\r\\n Tfull, Km(z−1) =\\r\\nPKm(z−1)Tc(z−1)\\r\\nSc(z−1) + Rc(z−1)(PKm(z−1) + Gp(z−1) −P0(z−1))\\r\\n                                                                                              (18)\\r\\nIn Fig. 11, the external reference href is mapped to the internal \\r\\nreference by\\r\\n                       hm, ref(z−1) = GT(z−1)href(z−1)                       (19)\\r\\nTherefore, the complete path from external reference to remote-\\r\\nnode pressure explicitly includes GT\\r\\n                      hm(z−1)\\r\\nhref(z−1) = GT(z−1)Tfull, Km(z−1)                      (20)\\r\\nAccordingly, the full closed-loop sensitivity pair is defined as\\r\\n                   Sfull, Km(z−1) = 1 −Tfull, Km(z−1)\\r\\nTfull, Km(z−1…778 tokens truncated…d τ ≈60 s are produced by imposed \\r\\ndisturbance steps in the equivalent head-loss channel; physically, they \\r\\nrepresent changes in hl driven by inlet-flow variations qin through \\r\\nEq. (3). For the identified set and for Km = 2.00, the controller remains \\r\\nbounded and rejects perturbations without steady-state offset, although \\r\\nthe stress-test case shows larger transient excursions and control effort \\r\\nthan the nominal case.\\r\\nConclusions\\r\\nThis paper presented a complete data-driven methodology for \\r\\npressure regulation in WDNs, from field measurements on a real \\r\\nsector to the design of a digital RST controller with Smith predictor. \\r\\nA low-order LTI model with delay for the hydraulic path between a \\r\\nPRV and a remote critical node was identified from operational data \\r\\nand step tests and validated against an independent 24-h consump-\\r\\ntion profile, with errors of about ±1 m H2O at the critical node. The \\r\\ncontroller was then validated in silico using real consumption data \\r\\nnot used during identification, providing an unbiased prediction of \\r\\nclosed-loop performance prior to field deployment.\\r\\nSimulation results under real demand show that the controller \\r\\nrejects demand disturbances and tracks reference changes with \\r\\n05026021-10\\r\\n© ASCE\\r\\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\\r\\n J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2026, 152(8): 05026021 \\r\\n Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by New Jersey Institute of Technology on 09/20/26. \\r\\nCopyright ASCE; all rights reserved, including rights for text and data mining and training of artificial intelligence or similar technologies. \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nUSER PAPER\\r\\n12 ---\\r\\nFig. 17. Closed-loop responses for identified and stress-test gains.\\r\\nzero steady-state error. A formal robustness assessment was addi-\\r\\ntionally performed for the Smith–RST architecture using the full \\r\\nclosed-loop path from hm, ref to hm. Over the tested uncertainty set \\r\\nKm ∈{0.75, 1.00, 1.26}, the worst sensitivity peaks are Ms = 1.846\\r\\nand Mt = 1.034 (Fig. 15). The Bode analysis of Tfull shows a \\r\\nmonotonic reduction of the frequency-domain reserves as gain \\r\\nincreases, with PM−3dB decreasing from 82.97° to 1.15° and \\r\\nGMTfull from 10.41 to 3.12 dB (Fig. 16). Time-domain responses \\r\\nwithin the identified set remain stable and non-oscillatory, with \\r\\npreserved disturbance rejection (Fig. 17); the additional stress-test \\r\\ncase Km = 2.00 (outside the identified set) remains stable but \\r\\nexhibits larger transient deviations and control effort, consistent \\r\\nwith reduced robustness reserve under stronger gain mismatch.\\r\\nThis robustness result is model-based and limited to the identi-\\r\\nfied uncertainty set; therefore, it should be interpreted as a rigorous \\r\\nverification of the implemented design, not as a full robust-control \\r\\nsynthesis (e.g., H∞).\\r\\nBeyond this case study, the main contribution is methodological. \\r\\nBy combining a static demand–headloss model and a low-order \\r\\ndynamic model identified from field data with an RST–Smith \\r\\npredictor controller, this paper provides a transparent, replicable \\r\\nframework that can be applied to other DMAs using standard instru-\\r\\nmentation and control hardware. In doing so, it helps bridge the gap \\r\\nbetween simulator-oriented RTC studies, proprietary commercial \\r\\nsolutions, and practical model-based procedures that expose PRV–\\r\\nnetwork dynamics and closed-loop performance.\\r\\nLimitations and Future Work\\r\\nThe present study is limited to in silico validation of the control-\\r\\nler using real consumption profiles, without yet deploying the \\r\\nalgorithm on the utility’s SCADA/PLC infrastructure. A full field \\r\\nimplementation is the next step and will be the subject of future \\r\\nwork. This will allow assessing additional performance indica-\\r\\ntors such as leakage reduction, energy savings, and operational \\r\\nrobustness, thereby complementing existing field case studies and \\r\\nproviding further evidence on the effectiveness of the proposed \\r\\nmethodology. Future work will focus on field implementation of the \\r\\nproposed methodology in operational DMAs, which is a necessary \\r\\nstep to fully bridge the gap between conceptual RTC frameworks \\r\\nand routine use in water utilities.\\r\\nData Availability Statement\\r\\nSome or all data, models, or code that support the findings of this \\r\\nstudy are available from the corresponding author upon reasona-\\r\\nble request.\\r\\nAcknowledgments\\r\\nThe authors would like to thank Aquaoccidente S.A. BattLeDIM_2022.txt
The Leakbusters team (Daniel et al. 2020, 2022) tackled the
challenge with a high-resolution pressure-driven method for leak-
age identification and localization composed of two sequential
modules. In the first module, linear regression models were cali-
brated using data with no leaks to predict pairwise sensor pressure
readings. When fed with new SCADA data, the reconstruction error
between predicted and observed readings was tracked to identify
the start time of a potential leak and the location of its nearest sen-
sor. The second module used the start time and most affected sen-
free approaches. The residuals, as well as other relevant time series,
can be analyzed using change detection techniques (e.g., CUSUM,
angle method), time series analysis and signal processing, empirical
method decomposition, regression analysis, hypothesis testing, and
other statistical approaches. More advanced statistical approaches,
such as machine learning and computational intelligence methods
based on fuzzy systems, have also been proposed.
A subset of methodologies considers optimization formulations,
which may rely on simulations to evaluate the objective functions
decomposition, spectral methods used at different stages of the algorithm
Statistical methods
Methods based on comparison with statistical distribution of the observed data, hypothesis testing, linear
regression, and so on
Machine learning and soft computing
Includes supervised/unsupervised machine learning (also feature engineering), fuzzy methods
Simulation-based optimization
Use of an optimization method with objective function based on simulation via hydraulic model

Kyriakou^{1} L14:   * cite8†Pavlos Pavlou^{1} L15:   * cite9†Mengning Qiu^{3} L16:   * cite10†Marios Polycarpou^{1} L17: \nL18: [Button: Show affiliations ]\nL19: \nL20:   * 1. KIOS Research and Innovation Center of Excellence\nL21:   * 2. Delft University of Technology\nL22:   * 3. Technion – Israel Institute of Technology\nL23:   * 4. Tsinghua University\nL24: ## Description\nL25: Drinking Water Distribution Networks (DWDN) are susceptible to infrastructure failures, which may lead to water losses. Typically, these water losses are due to background leakages and pipe bursts which may occur anywhere within the distribution network. Background leakages are normally difficult to detect due to their small size, whereas pipe bursts are easier to locate as they are of larger size and may appear on the surface.\nL26: The early detection and localization of some leakage event is extremely important, as this would reduce the time required for accommodating the event and therefore reducing the risk of further infrastructure degradation, contamination events and consumer complaints.\nL27: In previous years, a number of methodologies have been proposed to detect and isolate the location of leakage events using various types of sensor measurements. These methods were commonly evaluated on private commercial datasets, and as a result, it is not possible to objectively compare these methods in their ability to detect and isolate leaks.\nL28: In the past year, a leakage detection dataset has been proposed, LeakDB, based on benchmark networks and created using the WNTR tool, using pressure-driven demands and realistic leakage modelling. Inspired by the “BATtle of the Attack Detection ALgorithms” (BATADAL), which focused on the detection of cyber-physical attacks, our team decided to organize a similar “battle” focusing on leakage events.\nL29: The Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods (BattLeDIM),  aims at objectively comparing the performance of methods for the detection and localization of leakage events, relying on SCADA measurements of flow and pressure sensors installed within water distribution networks. Participants may use different types of tools and methods, including (but not limited to) engineering judgement, machine learning, statistical methods, signal processing, and model-based fault diagnosis approaches.\nL30: ## Files\nL31: \nL32: ###\nL33: \nL34: 2018_Fixed_Leakages_Report.txt\nL35: \nL36: cite11†Preview L37: ###\nL38: \nL39: Files (550.5 MB)\nL40: Name  | Size  |\nL41: ---
